The taker's decay gate takes the minimum of the moved `dirty_at`s; the donor's gate is left (too early at worst, its next walk recomputes). Reason: for the 64 KiB-slab medium classes a group is 64 to 128 slabs and the donor's current group is usually the one that is entirely `Empty` in mstress; refusing partly committed groups would have left the +21 MiB term in place.
- Phase 1.5 task 15 (2026-09-18): `reopen` and `on_live_zero` test the owner word under the class lock before touching the arena's sets. Reason: both carry a slab number from a lock-free step (`release_word`, `sub_live`) into the lock hold, and once groups can change hands another arena may adopt the slab in that gap and make it `Active`; "Active means ours" was an invariant before and is not now. Found by the new `concurrent_handoffs_keep_sets_and_descriptors_consistent` test (4 arenas, 8 threads: a slab of arena 2 in arena 3's partial set, then `slot ... belongs to arena 2 but was released through arena 3`), and by the Mac check_zero campaign as `class 7: slab 33 in the empty set is not empty` (1 in 200 runs of `stress 16 100 20` before the fix). Live slots pin a slab (counted in `live`), so nothing else crosses that gap; `debug_assert`s in `take` guard the sets' own popped slabs.
- Phase 1.5 task 15 (2026-09-18): `SlabTable::states` is an intrusive push-front list of every arena's `ClassState` over the table (`ClassState::next`), registered from `Arena::init` once the state has its final address; `SlabTable` keeps its 384-byte stride (`TABLE_TAIL` derived from the inner size). Reason: the taker needs the donor's `ClassState` to lock, `class.rs` must not depend on `arena.rs`, and a per-table pointer array indexed by arena would have committed a page per class.
- Phase 1.5 task 15 (2026-09-18, the measurement iteration): `group_handoff` defaults to `false`. Reason: on the protocol tier the handoff does not move the mstress row it was built for on either VM (x86_64 +0.2% RSS against old with overlapping five-round lists; aarch64 +3.4% against old and +2.4% against the same library with the key off, four of five runs above the control's largest, taking that row from +9.4% to +12.0% over jemalloc), and a default should not exercise the largest ownership change since task 4f for nothing; the code stays as the opt-in for the pattern it does serve (producer-consumer: xmalloc-test RSS 56 -> 45 MiB on aarch64, 53 -> 45 on x86_64, throughput within noise). The mstress row is accepted under the task's own fallback (the review's (c), the residual attributed with `arenasample`): what remains of the excess is `Active` at the peak (one partial slab per class per arena, the per-arena metadata, the 64 KiB-slab classes' current groups of 64..128 slabs, never whole-group idle under churn), which a whole-group handoff cannot take; a per-slab handoff would break the 4f line-ownership invariant and a cross-class dirty pool the per-class regions of design 3, so neither is attempted.
- Phase 1.5 task 15 (2026-09-18): the class tests turn the key on in `Env::with_lg_page` (both cfg variants), not per test. Reason: the static was `true` for every test before the flip; one call in the environment keeps the suite's behaviour, no test wants it off, and the static is process-wide under parallel tests.
- Phase 2 task 1 (2026-09-18): the guard fault test locates the guard slab from public `mallctl` keys and the address gap, not from a new diagnostic key. Reason: `negative.c sized_guard` already derives the geometry that way (`opt.guard_slab_interval`, `arenas.bin.<i>.slab_size`, `arenas.page`, the `heap::guard_interval_for` rule), a read-only key exposing slab numbers would be a second copy of the layout arithmetic to keep in step, and a test that reads internals proves less about what a C program can rely on. The `vmas` mode runs on every kernel (the Mac included) because the VMA bound is meaningful in both states and the program's own `madvise(MADV_GUARD_INSTALL)` probe decides whether guard slabs must be present; only the `fault` mode skips.
- Phase 2 task 1 (2026-09-18): the huge-run `PROT_NONE` guard fallback stays. Reason: its VMA cost is one VMA per live huge run (the `PROT_NONE` page between two committed runs keeps them separate; a guard-install kernel merges them), bounded by the number of live huge allocations rather than by committed bytes, whereas a `PROT_NONE` guard slab per eight 64 KiB slabs would be 2857 VMAs for the 714 MiB span the `vmas` test commits (measured 1 with guard regions); design 7 now says so. A huge-heavy program on a pre-6.13 kernel that approaches `vm.max_map_count` (65530) would need `guards:false`, which is the documented knob.

- Phase 2 task 4: `stats.cgroup.*` return `ENOENT` when nothing is tracked (no cgroup2, or `cgroup:false`) instead of `SIZE_MAX`/0 placeholders. Reason: jemalloc does the same for keys behind a disabled build option (the name resolves, the read says the facility is off), and a consumer must be able to tell "unlimited" from "not measured".
- Phase 2 task 4: the PSI trigger tries design 11's `some 150000 1000000` and, on `EINVAL`, the unprivileged `some 300000 2000000` (same 15% ratio, the smallest window a process without `CAP_SYS_RESOURCE` may ask for since kernel 6.4). Reason: the design's 1 s window is a privileged trigger on every kernel; probing both keeps the design's trigger where it is allowed and still arms in an unprivileged container. Any other error is recorded with its errno (`EROFS` under Docker's read-only cgroup mount) and the thread sleeps on its futex as before.
- Phase 2 task 4: the armed background thread sleeps in `ppoll` on the trigger and an eventfd that `wake` writes, instead of the futex; the eventfd is created once and never closed in the process that created it (a kicker that loaded the descriptor number must not find it reused), only a fork child closes its copy. Reason: a futex and a file descriptor cannot be waited on together, and the alternative (a capped futex sleep plus a zero-timeout poll) turns the trigger into polling.
- Phase 2 task 4: the limit walk is repeated at most once per second from the sampler. Reason: `docker update --memory` and Kubernetes in-place resizes change `memory.max` under a running process; two small reads per level per second is nothing, and `stats.cgroup.limit` stays truthful.
- Phase 2 task 4: `cpu.max` and `cpuset.cpus.effective` are parsed and read once at init (kept in `Cgroup` for design 15) because the parsers are twenty lines and host tested; nothing reads them yet.
- Phase 2 task 5 (2026-09-18), what retention means: the `Empty` bytes of every class of every arena plus the huge allocator's `Empty` extents (freed, dirty, not yet purged), not `Purged` (already out of RSS) and not cached slots (live to the kernel). Reason: it is the only memory the allocator holds that costs RSS without serving anything, so it is what a cgroup limit is spent on. The cap (5% of `min(high, max)`) applies in both bands under 90% (design 11 lists it only under 70%; a cap that vanished at 70% would let retention jump exactly where usage rises), and above 90% the zero period empties everything anyway.
- Phase 2 task 5: the cap is a keep ratio inside the existing curve walks (`allow = min(curve allowance, total * cap / retention)`, `policy::mul16`), not a separate purge pass. Reason: the curve keeps deciding which slabs go and in what order (design 8's semantics are untouched), the sum kept stays under the cap however retention is split across classes, and no new state is added to the `Empty` transition in `class.rs`. The huge walk never splits an extent; under the cap it purges the oldest extents whole until within the allowance (overshooting downward) instead of skipping one larger than the excess, because free neighbours coalesce and a program's 50 freed 8 MiB runs are one 400 MiB extent (measured: nothing purged in 3 s before this). The retention sum is taken by the evaluator under each class lock, only for classes whose lock-free `oldest_empty` is set and only when a limit exists; a mirror counter would have meant four more writes in `class.rs`'s slab-transition paths for a figure needed at most once per 10 ms.
- Phase 2 task 5: the policy is evaluated where a sample was actually taken (`purge::sample`: the periodic tick, the background thread's loop, a fired PSI trigger), never on the mallctl or report readers, which sample but do not apply. Reason: at most one evaluation per 10 ms process-wide, and the no-limit case is `limit == UNLIMITED` after two loads and a return, so a process without a limit pays nothing beyond task 4's sample; `Shared.policy` sits after `gc_interval_ns` so the tick's one extra `immediate` load hits a line it reads anyway.
- Phase 2 task 5: "purge immediately on `Empty`" above 90% is a zero effective period, applied at the next decay pass (the next periodic tick, or the background thread woken by a class's first `Empty` slab), and "caches GC every flush" is a zero GC interval in `Heap::tick` (GC at every periodic tick, 1/64 of flushes and refills). Reason: neither the `Empty` transition in `class.rs` nor `Heap::flush` is touched (both are on the hot-path list and need attribution to change), and the periodic tick already sits on every flush and refill path.
- Phase 2 task 5: `dirty_decay_ms:-1` stays never in every band. Reason: it is an explicit choice, as under jemalloc; a container that asks for no purging gets none, and the `cgroup policy:` line shows `decay_ms -1`. A period already at or under 1000 ms is not scaled either.
- Phase 2 task 5: the `memory.events` `high` baseline is the count at init (`Policy::new(cgroup.rises().0, ..)`). Reason: task 4's sampler counts a pre-existing `high` figure as one rise at discovery; without the baseline every process starting in a cgroup that had ever been reclaimed from would begin with a full purge.
- Phase 2 task 5: THP suppression is `thp` forced to `never` in the stored configuration at init when the effective limit at init is under 1 GiB (`policy::THP_MIN_LIMIT`), reported on the `opt.thp` and `cgroup policy:` lines. Reason: `MADV_HUGEPAGE` is not wired at all (the key is inert, design 10 defers it), so there is nothing else to suppress; whatever wires THP later reads the stored setting, and the decision is recorded here so that work does not re-derive it.
- Phase 2 task 5: `make test-cgroup-limit` runs `--test-threads=1`. Reason: `tests/c/cgroup_policy.c bands` fills the cgroup to 94% of the 512 MiB limit measured on `memory.current` (which charges every process in the container, the test harness included); a concurrent test touching 64 MiB (`cgroup.c plain`, the sampler test) would push the container over the limit and get something OOM-killed. The bands are measured at 80% and 94%, not 95%, to leave the harness 30 MiB of headroom.
- Phase 2 task 6: the churn test's idle phase is an idle event loop (4096 `malloc(48)`/`free` per 10 ms of sleep), not a bare sleep. Reason: without a background thread a decay walk runs only from the periodic tick and waits for the oldest `Empty` member to be one curve step old, so a process that frees and then allocates nothing keeps its memory under every setting and the test would say nothing about the policy; the trickle is identical with the policy on and off, is not another purge path (it is the path the policy uses), and with `cgroup:false` it runs the 10 s curve that keeps 79% at 2 s, which is the control the plan wants. Recorded in design 11.
- Phase 2 task 6: every container run gets `--memory-swap` equal to `--memory`. Reason: Docker Desktop's VM has 1 GiB of swap and `--memory` alone allows as much swap again, so a 400 MiB churn at 256m would spill into swap and `memory.current` would not mean what the policy reads; with swap off the 256m run scales to 200 MiB / 50 MiB (the plan's 400 MiB cannot be touched there) and the scaling is recorded in the results.
- Phase 2 task 6: `make test` gains `test-cgroup-churn` (four containers, ~40 s) and not the oscillation. Reason: the plan's 5-minute run cannot sit in the default suite; `make test-cgroup` and `make test-cgroup-oscillate` are the opt-in, and the ctests wrappers run both programs small on every profile so the sources stay compiled and exercised.
- Phase 2 task 6: the oscillation's control (`cgroup:false`) is run and recorded, not judged. Reason: the plan's criterion is the policy-on run; the control turned out to survive as well (challoc reuses `Empty` memory in place, so the peak is the same and only the low end differs), which is a finding about the test, not a failure, and is written into design 11 and the results file instead of being made a criterion it would not pass.
- Phase 2 task 3: the two push-time keys share one byte on `Shared`'s first line (`push_flags`: `PUSH_SCAN` = `double_free_scan`, `PUSH_ZERO` = `zero_on_free`) and the push tests the byte once (`Heap::push_cache_flags`: zero, the default, is the plain push; otherwise the clear and then the scan). Reason: the default fast path keeps exactly the one load and one predicted branch it had for `double_free_scan` (the byte replaces the bool at the same place), and the scan path is unchanged, so the detection-gaps price rows stay valid.
- Phase 2 task 3: `zero_on_free` clears before the push attempt, once, and the slow path (`free_slow_in`) does not clear again; on the sized path the class comes from the size hint, as the push already trusts it; a direct release (`MALLOCX_TCACHE_NONE`) clears the same way; a huge free clears the run's usable bytes (`Shared::zero_huge`, one extra huge-lock hold for the size) before `free_run`, since after the release another thread may own the pages. Reason: the class size is known on every path at that point and clearing a freed block twice or after its release would be either waste or a race.
- Phase 2 task 3: `slot_randomize` lives in `ClassState::claim_random` (same `plan`, random start word per non-prefix pick, Fisher-Yates over the batch) and is routed in `Heap::claim` from `Shared::config` on the slow path only; the generator is per heap (`Heap::rng`), seeded from `getrandom` on the first randomised claim (heap creation makes no syscall by default, unit-tested); direct claims (`MALLOCX_TCACHE_NONE`, heapless threads) stay address-ordered; with the key on, the compacting run is a shuffled block, so a scheduled pop serves a random slot of the lowest slab with room, not its lowest (documented in design 4). Reason: the smallest form that leaves the default refill and every fast path byte-for-byte alike and keeps `Claim::zeroed` exact (the shuffle changes order, not the set).
- Phase 2 task 3: the first form of `zero_on_free` (5c2c741) called the `memset` from inside the inlined push (`push_cache_flags`), and every default `free`/`sdallocx` paid for it in the prologue: a call inside the inlined fast path made the compiler save a callee-saved register (`sdallocx` frame 0x20 -> 0x50, `str x19`), +4.1 / +6.9 instructions per larson1 op and +4.7 / +10.1 per valkey1 op (aarch64 / x86_64, `quick-perf`), larson1 +1.9% and larson10 +2.3% on aarch64 in the 5-round `ab` with non-overlapping lists. 7087c44 makes the push decline under the key and `free_slow_in` (the cold path a full cache already takes) clear and push; the default `free`'s disassembly is back to the old loads (flag test ahead of the `len` test, no spill). Reason: the rule that the default fast paths stay byte-for-byte alike is what the disassembly diff (`objdump` of `free`, `sdallocx`, `malloc` old vs new, addresses masked) checks, and a slow-path call per free is inside the noise of a key that already costs a `memset` per free.
- Phase 2 task 3: `bench/quick.sh`'s gate compared the percentage as a string after `gsub` stripped `%` (`d > t` in awk), so a two-digit regression ("10.6" < "5" lexically) passed and was never confirmed; this iteration's x86_64 `quick` printed valkey1 +10.6% and "go". Fixed to `d+0 > t+0` at both sites (the confirm selection and the verdict). The 5-round `ab` is what judged that row (results file section 3).

- Phase 2.5 "floor and ceiling" (the human's direction, 2026-09-18, after Phase 2 was accepted): the next phase is docs/plan.md Phase 2.5, inserted before Phase 3, and the loop runs it in task order starting with task 1 (tcmalloc and Python in the comparison, one same-run four-way table). Reason: the floor (glibc 2.44, kernel 5.10, Wolfi's baseline) is claimed but has never run on a 5.10 or 64 KiB-page kernel, and the ceiling (runtime-probed kernel and CPU features: MTE, LAM, PAC, mm_cid per-CPU caches, mTHP) is the differentiator no shipped allocator has; Chainguard ships a recent kernel and can afford a CPU matrix on GCP. Phase 3's prerequisites (a) and (b) become Phase 2.5 task 3's cells.

- Phase 2.5 task 1b added (2026-09-19, the driving session): the flush sort found by task 1's attribution (M2) is a fast-path term with a per-operation attribution on three workloads, so it becomes a task before the feature report. Reason: the phase's goal is to be best on the hosts we ship; the rows where challoc trails mimalloc and tcmalloc by 10..30% on time share this one term, and the rule for fast-path changes (an attribution first) is already met.
- Phase 2.5 task 1b (2026-09-19), the form and why: the plan's first candidate (a selection, then a sort of the released half) and the bare selection both lost xmalloc-test by 21..51%, because the old sort's cost there was ~1.25k cycles per flush (ipnsort's O(n) path on already-sorted input, a consumer cache pushed in a producer's refill order) and a selection scrambles the kept half so the input never sorts again; an in-place bucket pass over the whole array (the plan's second candidate applied to everything) costs ~10 cycles per entry, the old sort's random-input cost, so xmalloc +49%. The landed form keeps an explicit `is_sorted` fast path (the old O(n) case, one pass stopping at the first inversion), the selection plus a slab bucket pass over the released half elsewhere, and sorts the kept half only on a cache's first two consecutive unsorted flushes and every 32nd after that (a one-byte counter, `unsorted_flushes`, in the cache header's padding): a cache pushed in order heals after one unsorted flush; one pushed in arbitrary order pays two half sorts, then one in 32. The unconditional kept sort (`quick` go on both VMs) gave back the whole gain and was rejected.
- Phase 2.5 task 1b (2026-09-19), acceptance reading: "glibc-simple and python dict_list-malloc improve by at least half of the sort's measured share on both VMs (same-run `ab`, five rounds)". glibc-simple: 8.81% / 8.44% share, `ab` -4.7% / -9.0%, met on both. dict_list-malloc is a `bench/python-bench.sh` row, not an `ab` row: measured as two back-to-back python-bench runs (REV=1339eb6, REV=HEAD, 5 rounds, disjoint lists), -6.7% on aarch64 against 7.8% needed (15.6% share) and -7.2% on x86_64 against 7.7% (15.3%), 1.1 and 0.5 points short; the `ab` row python1-malloc (json, share 5.3% / 4.7%) reads -3.0% (met) and -2.1% on the median (0.2 short; -3.6% on the means, two high outliers in the old list). The task is marked done on glibc-simple and on the term removed (the sort is gone; what remains, `flush` at 3.3..3.5% of glibc-simple, is the O(n) selection, and every form tried that avoids it cost more); the human can read the dict_list row the other way (Notes for the human).
- Phase 2.5 task 1b (2026-09-19), `release_owned` takes slab groups, not sorted batches: the design's "one atomic per 64 slots" came from consecutive same-word slots in a sorted batch; the release now accumulates a slab's bits in a `[u64; MAX_SLAB_WORDS]` on the stack (1 KiB for 8-byte slots in a 64 KiB slab) and clears them at the slab switch, the same atomics for any order inside a slab, and a duplicate inside a slab's group is caught on the scratch bit (any distance apart, where the old running mask caught only same-word neighbours and left the rest to the bitmap's clear-bit check, which still stands for a slab split across two groups). The negative mode `double_free_cached` now reports the boundary message (its two adjacent copies straddle the split under the sorted fast path); it aborts at the flush as before.

- Phase 2.5 task 2 (2026-09-19), `la57_enabled` on x86_64: `CR4.LA57` is not readable from user space and `cpuid` leaf 7 `ECX[16]` only says the CPU could; neither the reservation probe's addresses nor the top of `/proc/self/maps` can tell either, because a five-level kernel still places unhinted mappings and the stack below 2^47. The probe is a hinted `mmap(1 << 56, page, PROT_NONE, MAP_PRIVATE|MAP_ANONYMOUS|MAP_NORESERVE)` (one page, unmapped at once): a five-level kernel honours the hint and returns an address at or above 2^47, a four-level kernel ignores it. `/proc/cpuinfo`'s `address sizes: ... 57 bits virtual` is the cross-check in the container test.
- Phase 2.5 task 2 (2026-09-19), `cpuid` goes through an `asm!` block (`rbx` swapped through a scratch register, as the compiler reserves it) rather than `core::arch::x86_64::__cpuid_count`, per the phase's "runtime probes and `asm!`" wording; the two are the same instruction. Cache sizes come from the deterministic cache parameters (leaf 4, then `0x8000001D` when leaf 4 has no L1D entry, which is how AMD reports; no vendor-string logic) and only fall back to sysfs when those give nothing, so the C4 reads no sysfs file. The arm64 line size comes from `CTR_EL0.DminLine` and sysfs is read only for the sizes.
- Phase 2.5 task 2 (2026-09-19), the sysfs cache reader reads six files, not twelve: each sysfs open/read/close costs about 6 us in the VM's container, and the first form (`level` and `type` for four indexes plus `size` and `coherency_line_size`) put `Cpu::probe` at 56 us against the task's 50 us budget. The reader now takes `index0` as the first level-1 leaf (cacheinfo fills leaves level by level, data before instruction; `probe_matches_proc_cpuinfo` asserts `index0/level` is 1 and its type Data or Unified on every host it runs on, so the matrix of tasks 3 and 4 checks the assumption per kernel), reads its `type` and `size`, then `index1/level` and, if that is not 2, `index2/{level,type,size}`; `coherency_line_size` only when the registers gave no line. 37 us on the C4A.
- Phase 2.5 task 2 (2026-09-19), the feature line is built once at init into a 512-byte static (`FeatureLine`, `fmt::Write` over a fixed buffer, NUL-terminated) and both `challoc_features()` and `malloc_stats_print` hand out those bytes; the fork child rebuilds it after re-probing the purge backend so `process_madvise=` stays truthful there. Keys are only added, never renamed; sizes print as `64K`/`2M`/bytes, `unknown` where no source had a figure (the Mac's Docker VM has the cache directories without `size` files). `cache_source` (cpuid, sysfs, none) is kept in the struct for the results files but is not a key.
- Phase 2.5 task 2 (2026-09-19), the host fixtures: the Axion, the Mac VM and the 8581C words are captured on the hosts; the Altra (Neoverse N1) HWCAP word and the Zen 4 leaf 7 registers are composed from their published `/proc/cpuinfo` flag lists and labelled as such in `cpu.rs`; task 4's matrix captures them.

- Phase 2.5 task 3 (2026-09-19), `process_madvise` needs kernel 6.13, not 5.10: the matrix found `process_madvise=no` on 5.10, 6.1 and 6.8 (x86_64 and arm64) even under Docker `--privileged`, and `pidfd_self` on 7.0.0-31-generic privileged. The syscall exists since 5.10 but accepts `MADV_DONTNEED` only from 6.13 (COLD/PAGEOUT/WILLNEED/COLLAPSE before), so the batched purge is a 6.13+ path and the `madvise` loop is the path on every older kernel. Design 17's table and README's kernel table said "5.10 with a pidfd of self"; corrected (an actual error, per the rule). The probe itself was right all along: it tries and records the error.
- Phase 2.5 task 3 (2026-09-19), the test modules take the kernel's page size: `huge.rs`, `heap.rs`, `purge.rs`, `class.rs`, `meta.rs` and `stats.rs` tests had `PAGE = 4096` and built real `PROT_NONE` reservations with it; on the 64 KiB cell that was 58 failures ("fake page must be a real page multiple", unaligned commits). Each module now has a `page_size()` helper (the kernel's on Linux, 4 KiB in the synthetic host environment), the huge test region is 16384 pages rather than 64 MiB, expectations are written in pages (a 1 MiB run is 257 pages of 4 KiB and 17 of 64 KiB), the class tests' metadata scales with the page (every table reservation is page-rounded) and the tests that fake a 16 KiB page skip when the real page is larger (they simulate exactly that case). Nothing outside `#[cfg(test)]` changed for it. `purge_never_races_a_claim` runs until the purger has purged once (bounded by 30 s) instead of assuming it gets a CPU inside a 4 ms window (failed once on a 4-vCPU cell).
- Phase 2.5 task 3 (2026-09-19), the cells as they are, not as named in the plan: Ubuntu 24.04's GCE image boots `7.0.0-1011-gcp` (the reference kernel), so the 6.8 GA cells install `linux-generic` and the image-default cells are `gcp-*`; `linux-generic-hwe-24.04` is 7.0.0-31-generic, which is the 6.13+ non-GCE cell (no Fedora family on GCE, only CoreOS); the 5.10 cell uses the newest READY image of the deprecated `debian-11` family (bullseye's LTS ended 2026-08-31, so provisioning drops its security suite and treats `git` as optional). The 7.0 reference cell is cited from the reference VMs and also ran on the small machines as `gcp-*`. The `--privileged` runs (k68-amd64 and hwe-amd64, plus gcp-amd64) stand in for the plan's bare-metal or nested-KVM cell; they say so in the results file.
- Phase 2.5 task 3 (2026-09-19), the matrix driver's mechanics worth keeping (all found by a failing cell): a `prep` kernel must clear `GRUB_FORCE_PARTUUID` (GCE's Ubuntu boots initrd-less; a generic kernel panics on the NVMe root without its initrd) and be selected by menu title (the gcp version string sorts above); wait for a new `boot_id`, not merely for ssh; Debian's `core_pattern=core` leaves a root-owned core in the bind-mounted tree after the negative tests and breaks the next `docker build`, so provisioning discards cores with one `sysctl -w` (never `sysctl --system`, which re-applies GCE's `ip_forward = 0` over Docker's); matrix ssh keeps no host keys because GCE recycles external IPs within minutes; a cell's EXIT trap must not reference a function's locals; two drivers with one prefix sweep each other's VMs (`CHALLOC_MATRIX_PREFIX`).

- Phase 2.5 task 4b added (2026-09-19, the driving session): the valkey1 gap on Sapphire Rapids, Genoa, Milan and Cascade Lake found by the CPU matrix (+17..+39 points against the reference CPU) is attributed before task 5. Reason: the phase's goal is to be best on the hosts Chainguard ships, and these are the CPUs customers run; a row that is at parity on the reference VM and 40% behind elsewhere means the reference VMs have been hiding a mechanism, and the rule is attribution first.

- Four decisions delegated to the driving session by the human ("Your call on those", 2026-09-19): (1) the 64 KiB-page container test keeps task 3's 8 MiB envelope, and plan task 4d measures the real consumer (the Valkey soak on a 64 KiB-page VM) before the sub-page slab geometry is called fine or fixed; (2) tagged pointers: task 5 (design and probes) proceeds, task 6 (implementation) is gated on a host that exposes MTE or LAM, none on GCP, a bare-metal AmpereOne first; the phase does not wait for it; (3) `group_handoff` stays off by default (no target consumer benefits, aarch64 mstress read 2.4% worse with it on, it weakens the line-ownership invariant); per-CPU caches (task 8) is where the same term is attacked properly and the default is re-examined; (4) the mstress x86_64 RSS row stays accepted per task 15, re-measured under task 8. Reason: each follows the phase's rules (measure the target consumer first, never trade a hot invariant for a benchmark row, do not block on hardware we cannot get). Also: task 4c (the sized path's division and instruction budget, from task 4b's attribution) goes ahead of tasks 5 to 13.

- Phase 2.5 task 4e (2026-09-19), the form of the page-sized slab geometry: the class table rewritten in place at init (`CLASSES` as `BASE_CLASSES` in a `Sync` `UnsafeCell` at a fixed address; init writes `lg_slab`, `nslots`, `slab_bits` of the raised classes, never `size` or `recip`), not a pointer to one of two tables on `Shared` and not a shift where `lg_slab` is read. Reason: zero fast-path cost by construction (the exported fast paths disassemble identically; a pointer would put a dependent load in front of the sized path's only geometry load). Cost accepted: the table is in writable memory like the `Regions` the same paths trust, and LLVM keeps Rust's division checks on `/ nslots` in a few slow paths (per refill or rebuild, one `cbz` each). The rule caps at 64 KiB (`min(lg_page, LG_SMALL_SLAB)`): every table slab below 64 KiB belongs to the 512..1024 B classes, so this is exactly "no slab smaller than the page" on 4, 16 and 64 KiB kernels, and a page above 64 KiB (no supported kernel) keeps the page groups. `Regions::{nslabs, slab_addr, slot_addr}` stopped being `const fn` (they read the runtime table); `config::ladder` reads `BASE_CLASSES` (sizes are constant). The 64 KiB soak's remaining failure (`active`) is opened as plan task 4f rather than chased here: it is a compaction term at equal geometry and needs its own attribution.

- Phase 2.5 task 4g (2026-09-19): no change to the compacting cache's gate, rebuild or flush at 64..128-region slabs, and design 13.3's sentence that the defragmenter relies on `MALLOCX_TCACHE_NONE` to land allocations in the fullest slabs is replaced by what the code does (the lowest partial slab, as jemalloc's `slabcur`; Valkey's rule only needs the destination outside the below-average slabs). Reason: the probe drains at this geometry (469 -> 214..216) and the soak's residue is values the churn does not free at 4..5 per slab in every slab, which no cache policy moves; the 13.3 sentence was an actual error (4f measured where the copies land) and the task text leaned on it as a candidate.
- Phase 2.5 task 4f (2026-09-19): no allocator-side change for the 64 KiB post-delete `active` gap. Reason: every input challoc gives Valkey's decision rule is exact and its `MALLOCX_TCACHE_NONE` placement matches jemalloc's; the gap is one defragmenter cycle that Valkey's absolute `active-defrag-ignore-bytes` (100 MB) grants to the allocator whose second pass leaves more waste (jemalloc's 192..448 KiB slabs), so the honest levers are Valkey's constant (the human's call; the 2026-09-17 rule forbids a `USE_CHALLOC` value for the soak RSS criterion) or making challoc waste more, which is not a fix. The task closes as an attribution with the criterion FAIL stated; the second-order churn-drift term is left for a task of its own.

- Phase 2.5 task 4f's four options, the driving session's call (2026-09-19): no `USE_CHALLOC` value for `active-defrag-ignore-bytes` (the 2026-09-17 rule: never to pass the soak RSS criterion); the 64 KiB post-delete row is recorded as measured with its attribution (Valkey stops both defragmenters on an absolute 100 MB constant; jemalloc's 192..448 KiB slabs leave more waste, re-cross it and get a third cycle, challoc's 64 KiB slabs purge and plateau under it; at 80 MB challoc lands at +1.9%), and the one allocator-side lever the attribution exposed, the compacting cache draining 4 MiB where jemalloc's churn drains 12 at 64..128-region slabs, becomes plan task 4g. Reason: the rule stands, the row is not a challoc defect, and the compacting cache at this geometry is challoc's own to fix.

- Phase 2.5 task 7 (2026-09-19): the modifier for a cache entry's signature is the stack's base address (`CacheHeader::slots`), not the entry's slot address or the header's: it is loaded by every pop and push anyway, it lets entries move within the stack signed (the scheduled pop's swap, the rebuild's rotation, the flush's selection), and it survives `thread.arena`'s swap of whole header arrays. The heap pointer's modifier is the thread pointer (`tpidr_el0`, already loaded by the TLS accessor). The flag the fast paths branch on is a third bit of `Shared::push_flags` (`PUSH_PAC`), which `malloc` now loads too (`adrp; ldrb` from the static's line); `existing_heap` takes the `Shared` as a raw pointer and reads that byte only after the TLS slot proved init complete. The flush orders signed entries by their address bits (`pac::mask()` from one `xpacd`) and authenticates only the released half; the form that authenticated the whole stack and re-signed the kept half cost xmalloc 15.8% on the C4A. The corruption test is a Rust test in a forked child (private field access, no debug export), not a `MALLOC_CONF` debug key: a mallctl or key that scribbles a cache entry would be a write primitive in the shipped library.
- Phase 2.5 task 7 (2026-09-19): the flush takes the keyed sorts (`*_by_key` with the address mask) only when signing is on; with the key the identity they still cost every host xmalloc 5.3% (the comparator closure loses the primitive pointer sort's specialisations), which was the whole of the flag-off binary's gate failure. Two copies of the fast path selected once at the entry point were tried and rejected (LLVM merged the tails and gave `malloc` a frame); the single path with a `tbz` per `autda` and `hint::assert_unchecked` on the heap pointer is what ships.
- Phase 2.5 task 7 (2026-09-19): `pac_pointers` ships off by default (the plan's own fallback): the second form is +11.0% on xmalloc and +1.2..3.0% on the other C4A rows in `quick`, over the 5% gate and over Phase 2 task 3's 1% rule for a default-on hardening; the floor is two PAC instructions per cached entry (sign in, authenticate out) and one `autda` per call for the heap pointer. The unit tests build their `Shared` with the key on, so the signed path runs in every heap test on a PAC host.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): jemalloc's own `percpu_arena` spellings are
  accepted as aliases, `disabled` as `off` and `phycpu` as `percpu`, the
  way `thp` accepts `default` as `never`. Reason: until this commit the key
  was accepted with any value, so a Valkey or Redis conf string written
  against jemalloc (`percpu_arena:phycpu,abort_conf:true`) parsed clean;
  rejecting those spellings now would make `abort_conf` abort a
  configuration that worked yesterday. `phycpu` maps to `percpu` as the
  nearest mode (a concurrency id is not a physical CPU; no hyperthread
  pairing exists here). Anything else (`true`, `on`, mixed case) is a bad
  value, reported and left at `auto`.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): under a failed probe `percpu` and `auto`
  behave the same (thread caches); the only difference is the report note
  (`PercpuArena::forced`). Reason: a forced request cannot read an `mm_cid`
  field the kernel does not write, and the plan says the fallback holds
  with a note, not an abort.
- Phase 2.5 task 10 (lane B): the coordinator's candidate (a) (switch to
  `MADV_DONTNEED` when `memory.current` stays above the band after a lazy
  pass) did not match the mechanism: the band really was `Low` after the
  first eager walk and the mirror was fresh. The rule taken is about the
  cap: an excess over the retention cap is returned with the advice of
  the band it was first seen in, eager until the retention is within the
  cap, because the cap is a promise about resident, charged memory that
  a lazy purge does not keep; an excess first seen under 70% (the lazy
  test) stays lazy. Alternatives not taken: "the cap is always eager"
  (would have made the lazy container test and the plan's task 10
  wording wrong: 200 MiB freed at 39% would return eagerly); candidates
  (b)/(c) (probe or gate on `memory.stat` `lazyfree`), because no kernel
  uncharges `MADV_FREE`d pages before reclaim and the line exists on 5.10
  and 6.1 too, so neither distinguishes the cells. Task 10's results
  file (`2026-09-19-lane-b-two-advice-purge.md`) said the 512m churn ran
  "at 89.8% usage, scaled band, eager": it runs at 79% and its eager
  passes were one-tick luck on the 16-vCPU VMs; the new file says so.
- Phase 2.5 task 9 (lane B): `always` on a PMD over 2 MiB (16 and 64 KiB
  pages) is the folio path, not a refusal of the key: an operator asking
  for `always` on a 64 KiB host with `hugepages-2048kB` enabled gets
  2 MiB folios on 2 MiB slabs and up, which is the useful reading; with
  nothing below the PMD enabled the mode is off and the report says why
  (`PmdTooLarge`). Huge runs of 512 MiB or more are not advised in that
  case (mode off): a 512 MiB THP per run is not a saving anyone asked
  for. `auto` is unchanged at 64 KiB (the PMD gating rule applies as at
  4 KiB).
- Phase 2.5 task 16 (lane B): the never-advice is gated on the *key*
  (`never`, or the forced `never` of design 11), not on `Mode::Off`: an
  `auto` the kernel's sysfs table made inert (no sub-PMD size, or the
  PMD madvise-gated) keeps its regions eligible for the folios the host
  may enable later, since `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` forbids mTHP folios as well
  as PMD pages. Consequence, recorded in design 17: on an `always` host
  `auto`'s inert regions and `always`'s unadvised ones still get the
  host's PMD pages (the huge run under `auto` shows 8192 kB there). The
  second half of the task, designed here and not implemented (it needs a
  config key, lane C's `config.rs`): a key `thp_process` with values
  `default` (nothing) and `advised`; under `advised` init calls
  `prctl(PR_SET_THP_DISABLE, 1, PR_THP_DISABLE_EXCEPT_ADVISED)` (6.18+,
  probed by the call's return: `EINVAL` below) before the reservations,
  so the process gets huge pages only where challoc `MADV_HUGEPAGE`d
  (`auto`'s folios, `always`'s chunks, huge runs), whatever the host's
  global mode; the feature line gains `thp_process=<mode>` and the
  report's `thp:` line shows the host's global mode (`sys::features::Thp
  .enabled`, already read) and whether the prctl is in force; the
  both-ways container test flips the global mode as `tests/c/thp.c`
  does and checks AnonHugePages of an unadvised region under `auto`.
  Off by default because it is process-wide (it changes what the host's
  `always` means for every mapping of the process, libc's and the
  program's own included), and `never` does not need it: the
  `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` half covers challoc's regions on every kernel.
  Chainguard OS ships `always` with the 2 MiB size on `inherit`: with
  this commit `thp:never` (the default) means never there; the cgos
  four-way RSS rows (plan task 14, lane E) should be re-read at or after
  71f54e0.
- Phase 2.5 task 12 (lane B): the drop is decided on the gated rows' attribution (xmalloc-test and the Valkey model), not on the soak, which was flat either way; the soak's one-round throughput (+1..4% for new) and RSS after settle (+6.5%) are inside its run-to-run spread and the sidecar counters did not move. The alternative outcome (keep it because the io-threads soak gained 1..4% in one round) was not taken because the same counters say the gain is not the hint's. The module stays in the tree with an unused `demote` so the next attempt (a hint issued only where `Heap::release_routed` knows the release is cross-arena, heap.rs, lane A's file) is one call plus a measurement.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane B): `rseq=yes` requires the kernel to have written the area (`cpu_id >= 0`), not just a non-zero `__rseq_size`, so a registration glibc believes in but the kernel refused (`-2`) reads as no rseq and no `mm_cid`; `rseq_size` still prints the symbol's value so the two can be told apart. The three keys sit in the kernel section (after `collapse`), since the registration is a kernel-plus-libc fact rather than a CPU one, and `tests/c/features.c` (a task 2 test file, outside the lane's `src/` partition) was extended with the three keys so its every-key-once count keeps passing.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): default `slice_ext:auto`, by the plan's rule (within noise or better on both VMs: it is, on `quick`, `quick-perf`, `ab` and the same binary on/off oversubscribed). No benefit was measurable either: the rows the task named have almost no holder preemption (the slow-path hold is a few dozen instructions and the rows run at one thread per vCPU), and no GCE Ubuntu or arm64 kernel passes the probe today, so `auto` changes nothing on the reference VMs or Chainguard OS and only switches on for an x86_64 host whose kernel enables the option. The human decides with the numbers in `bench/results/2026-09-19-x86_64-lane-c-task15-slice-ext.md` section 9; `off` is one key away.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): the enable is per task, not per process (the plan said "per process"; `rseq_slice_extension_prctl` sets `current->rseq.slice.state.enabled`, `rseq_fork` resets a `CLONE_VM` child's rseq state and copies it to a plain fork child; confirmed on 7.0.14 by `tests/c/slice_ext.c`: 32 new threads at `prctl(GET) == 0` before their first allocation, 32 at 1 after, the fork child at 1). So init enables only the init thread and every other thread is enabled lazily by `slice::acquired` on its first lock hold, keyed on bit 5 of its own `flags` (the same cache line as `slice_ctrl`, so the check is one load per hold); a `prctl` that fails switches the process off instead of retrying a syscall per hold. Nothing needs re-enabling in a fork child.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): `on` follows PercpuArena's `percpu` shape, not `spin_hint`'s `Issue::Unavailable`: a kernel without the option is the documented fallback, not a configuration error, so `on` with a failed probe is not an issue (no stderr line, no `abort_conf` abort), requests nothing (a field the kernel does not read cannot be forced), and is noted in the report line (`; slice_ext:on requested, unavailable`). `opt.slice_ext` reads back `on`.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): every `RawLock` gets the request (class, table, huge, metadata, statistics, background), not a chosen subset: they are all slow-path holds and the per-hold cost did not register. Nested holds (the fork handlers, heap creation under the cache-table lock) request per lock and the inner release withdraws the request, so the outer hold goes on unextended; a per-thread depth counter would need TLS the lock module does not have and the loss is only the extension.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): where `rseq_slice_yield` returns `ENOSYS` but bit 4 is set (Docker's default seccomp profile hides every syscall newer than its table while letting the `prctl` through; found on the C4 with the custom kernel: `ENOSYS` in the container, 0 with `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined`), the grant is ended with `sched_yield` instead of turning the extension off: any syscall entry inside a grant revokes it and reschedules, and the grant path is rare (17 per 10^9 operations). The report says which syscall is in use. The plan's "use the dedicated one" holds wherever it reaches the kernel.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): `slice::acquired_area` (the release's pointer) is keyed on "the probe has passed" rather than "the extension is on", so a hold that stored its request before another thread switched the process off (a failed `prctl`) still withdraws it.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): the unit test for the granted path runs a CPU hog per CPU beside the 4x-CPU lock threads: with only futex sleepers as competition a holder alone on its CPU is never preempted (0 grants in 3 s on the custom kernel with `--test-threads=1`; 12 with the rest of the suite running beside it), and the assertion `grants > 0` needs runnable competition to be deterministic.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): the scheduler is modelled by what the caches can observe, not as a persistent cid per thread. The first version carried `cid: Option<u8>` per thread with deschedule/schedule steps at every state; it multiplied every protocol state by the 13 thread-to-cid assignments and every per-CPU scenario passed 5 M states. Now the `mm_cid` a section reads at its entry is the exploration's choice (any cid: the kernel may have moved the thread anywhere since its last section) and, since running threads of one process never share an `mm_cid`, a thread with an outstanding read of that cid was descheduled in between: under `Rseq` it is aborted to its section's start (the locals and the cid dropped, so the state equals the one before its entry), under `None` it goes on stale, under `CidOutside` the abort handler keeps the index read before the section. A thread inside a section may also be preempted spontaneously (the one explicit scheduler step, `Op::Sched` in a trace). A preemption outside a section is unobservable to the caches except as the cid going to another thread's section, which the entry models. Sound for every cache-observable behaviour; the `strict_cid` property is therefore "the thread lost the cid it read before its commit" rather than a literal compare of two cid values.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): under `Section::Rseq` the speculative reads and the commit store are one step (`after_reads`), the entry a step of its own. The kernel lets no other thread run on the cid until the section ends and one that does aborts this one, so nothing the cache can observe interleaves between the reads and the store; it is the module's existing rule for lock holds. The buggy variants keep the store separate, which is where a stale read commits. Measured: two threads of `[Pop, Push]` 23619 -> 17179 states; three threads with two pushes each stay past 2 M, so two of the three threads of `percpu_share` only pop and `percpu_migrate`/`percpu_purge` have two threads.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): dead locals are reset when a machine ends (`Thread::clear_locals`: `picks`, `cur`, the `rel_*` fields, the section's `spec_*`, `sec_cid`, `stale`), so states that differ only in dead locals are one memoisation key and an aborted section returns to exactly its pre-entry state. This changed the recorded counts of task 13's four scenarios (2153 -> 943, 11214 -> 8427, 5759 -> 4271, 59925 -> 42047) with the same verdicts and the same printed interleavings; before the canonicalisation the refactor of `step` into `step_refill`/`step_release` reproduced the four counts exactly (2153, 11214, 5759, 59925), which is the evidence the thread-mode model is unchanged. Plan task 13's paragraph now carries both sets.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): the model's `Release` routes each slab to its owner word's arena (`Heap::release_routed`) instead of aborting when the owner is not the thread's arena; a per-CPU cache holds slots of another cid's arena after a migration, and the real code routes. The abort that remains is a slab never handed out (`SlabTable::owner` past the watermark). A reopen runs per owner run, as `release_owned` does.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): `Held` lost its `from` field (the cache a slot was popped from) to keep owned slots canonical; `Outcome::cross_pushes` counts pushes of a slot whose slab's owner arena is not the cache's cid, which is exactly the case that makes the flush route to another arena. `Purge` in the per-CPU mode runs on the arena the scenario names for the thread (decay is an arena's work, not a CPU's). The exploration runs on a scoped thread with a 1 GiB stack: the depth-first path is one frame per step with a `Node` clone, and a per-CPU path overflowed the 2 MiB test-thread stack.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane C): `CACHE_CAP` is 2 and a refill claims 2 slots (one handed out, one pushed back) where the real code claims `cap / 2`: the model wants a push-back and a flush in every scenario at the smallest cache.
- Phase 2.5 acceptance (lane D): the task 8 campaign puts challoc alone
  under a key through `CHALLOC_CONF` (read by challoc after `MALLOC_CONF`,
  by no stock allocator), which `DOCKER_BASE` in the Makefile now forwards
  into every container when set, so the same suite (Valkey soak, Ruby
  rows, `test-all`, Ruby soak, container tests) runs with jemalloc's column
  untouched; only `ab` uses the variant's own `MALLOC_CONF`
  (`percpu=tree:percpu_arena:percpu` next to `default=tree` in one run).
  Reason: the suites take one environment for every allocator, and a
  `MALLOC_CONF` value would also reach jemalloc, which has a `percpu_arena`
  key of its own.
- Phase 2.5 acceptance (lane D): the "many idle threads" Ruby soak is a
  variant of `bench/ruby/soak.rb` (`IDLE_THREADS`, threads that allocate a
  burst once and then a tiny one every 5..10 s) rather than a new script,
  and `larson64` a new `lib.sh` row (64 threads on 16 CPUs), so the percpu
  half compares like with like against the baseline half. Baseline shape:
  64 threads make `Arenas: 64` and 415..463 MiB against jemalloc's
  115..192 MiB at t=300.
- Phase 2.5 acceptance (lane D): the kernel matrix re-run logs are named
  `bench/results/matrix/2026-09-19-lane-d-acceptance-<cell>.log`
  (`MATRIX_DATE` override) so task 3's records of the morning stay intact;
  lab cells run under `CHALLOC_MATRIX_PREFIX=challoc-lane-d-lab` so the
  driver's exit sweep of the matrix prefix does not delete them.
- Phase 2.5 task 17 (lane D): the `memory.peak` reset lives in a sidecar
  (`bench/probes/cgpeak.c`) that holds one file description for the run,
  because the 6.12 reset is scoped to the description that wrote it (a
  shell's `echo reset > memory.peak` followed by `cat` shows the birth peak
  again; measured on 7.0), and it is driven by the phase file the Valkey soak
  already writes, with an acknowledgement so the phase's load starts after
  the reset. Reason: the alternative (a host-side reset triggered from the
  container) needs a second channel; the sidecar reuses the one the soak has.
- Phase 2.5 task 17 (lane D): the write happens inside the container, made
  possible by `make ... CGROUP_PEAK=1` (`--cgroupns=host` and a read-write
  bind mount of `/sys/fs/cgroup`), opt-in and probed by the write's success,
  never on by default: Docker mounts the tree read-only and the nsdelegate
  boundary refuses the write from inside a cgroup namespace, and the flags
  change the container's view of its cgroup path (`0::/system.slice/...`
  instead of `0::/`), which the container tests should not see unasked. The
  campaign, which runs on the VM host, reads the fresh containers'
  `memory.peak` from the host instead (no reset needed for a fresh cgroup).
- Phase 2.5 task 17 (lane D): the Valkey soak builds the jemalloc server
  before any soak runs and the sidecar records the cgroup's page cache
  (`memory.stat file`) at each phase end, after the first run of the column
  charged a ~300 MiB build cache to jemalloc's phases; the cgroup peak counts
  every process of the container and its page cache, which is the figure the
  plan asked for, and the header now says so with the numbers.
- Phase 2.5 task 17 (lane D): `tests/c/pagemap_guard.c` expects
  `PAGE_IS_SWAPPED` beside `PAGE_IS_GUARD` on a guard page (the kernel's
  `pagemap_page_category` classifies the guard marker as a swap-type PTE
  first: `0x112` on 7.0), and its 6.13..6.15 fallback checks "not present,
  a swap-type entry" rather than "neither present nor swapped"; every
  capability is probed by the ioctl's own answer (ENOTTY, EINVAL on an
  unknown category bit), never by a kernel version.
- Phase 2.5 task 17 (lane D): the guard test is a Makefile target
  (`test-pagemap-guard`, hooked into `make test` after the cgroup targets)
  rather than a `src/api/ctests.rs` driver, because lane D edits nothing
  under `src/`.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: the Makefile stays untouched on a bootc host; a `~/bin/docker` shim runs `sudo podman` and adds `--network=host` to `run` and `build` (netavark has no `nft` on the host, rootless podman has no `/run/containers/storage`), so every `make` target of the repository works unchanged and the cell exercises the same commands as the Docker cells. Reason: the smallest change with no merge surface in the Makefile; the host-network side effect (redis on the host's 6379) is harmless on a throwaway VM.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: `/var` is grown online with `resize2fs` from a `wolfi-base` container that sees the mount (`-v /var:/mnt/var`) instead of attaching a scratch disk. Reason: the image ships the 49 GiB partition already grown and only the filesystem small (OS-3363); two seconds, no reboot, no extra disk to delete.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: detached VM jobs on Chainguard OS run as `sudo systemd-run --uid=<login> ...` transient system units, not `setsid nohup`. Reason: the first image build died with the ssh session (the session scope is ended at logout on this image; `Linger=no`, so a `--user` unit would die too). The driver's cgos launch uses it; the Docker cells keep `setsid nohup`.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: the cgos cell writes `bench/results/scorecard-cgos-<arch>.md` (not `scorecard-c4a.md` / `scorecard-c4.md`, the same machine families): the per-CPU files record the Ubuntu 7.0 kernel with THP `madvise`, this one a 6.18 kernel with THP `always`, and a line for the same commit must not replace the other's.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: 3 rounds for the cgos four-way (the plan's table is 5) and the cells' scorecard: 5 rounds would have been about 35 minutes for the `ab` step alone per host and the shapes are 8 vCPU, so the file says 3 and the comparison with Ubuntu is by percentages and mechanism, never absolute.
- Phase 2.5 task 14: the four-way's container rows take the cell's protocol run (`make test-cgroup`) and the post-run repetition is quoted below them, so a reader sees both the passing protocol value and the stall frequency instead of one or the other.
- Phase 2.5 task 16 (lane C): the key is `thp_process`, values `default`
  and `advised`, following the plan text. `default` means "nothing
  issued", not "the kernel's default": the process keeps whatever policy
  it inherited (a parent's `PR_SET_THP_DISABLE` included), so
  `thp_process:default` is byte for byte the allocator without the key.
  No `never`/`always` values: a process-wide `PR_SET_THP_DISABLE` of 1
  without the except-advised flag would also disable the pages `thp:auto`
  advises, and `always` is not something `prctl` can grant; if lane B
  wants the plain disable as a value later, `ThpProcess` gains a variant
  and `requested()` stays the "issue a prctl" gate. The enum is stored on
  `Config` next to `thp`, not folded into `Thp`, because the two compose
  (`thp:auto,thp_process:advised` is the Chainguard OS configuration the
  plan aims at) and because `thp:default` already means `never` for
  jemalloc compatibility.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane C): `prefault` is a plain bool with no
  cross-key validation at parse time (no `Issue` when
  `background_thread` is false): the parser is per pair and the last
  pair wins in either order, so a "prefault needs the thread" report
  would depend on key order; instead `Config::prefault_active()` gives
  lane B the one predicate to gate the pass on and the report can say
  `prefault: requested, no background thread` from it. `KEYS` order puts
  `prefault` right after `background_thread` (the report reads in that
  order) and `thp_process` right after `thp`; `opt.` mib indices of the
  later keys shift by one and two, which nothing pins (the mallctl tests
  compute them from `KEYS`).
- Phase 2.5 task 14 (lane E): `cgos72-amd64` is in `MATRIX_CGOS_CELLS`
  (the three cells run concurrently under `MATRIX_JOBS=3`, so `matrix
  cgos` costs the same wall time) and writes its own
  `scorecard-cgos72-amd64.md` rather than the 6.18 file: one trend file
  per kernel, distinguishable without reading the note column.
- Phase 2.5 task 14 (lane E): the cell keeps the podman shim on the
  workstation image although that image ships Docker 29.8.1 with
  `dockerd` running, so the three Chainguard OS hosts run one container
  runtime; a Docker variant of the cell is a one-line provisioning change
  if wanted.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane B, 2026-09-20), what a committed-but-unclaimed
  prefaulted slab is to `next_slab` (lane C asked, for the model op): it is
  a `Purged` slab in the arena's `purged` set, state byte `Purged`,
  `dirty_at` 0, populated before it is published and while the class lock
  is held. Reasons, in order: (1) `next_slab` already takes the lowest
  `Purged` slab right after the lowest `Empty` one and before committing
  anything, so the refill takes the prefaulted slab with no change to the
  claim code; (2) a `Purged` slab reads zero, which a fresh populated slab
  does, so `take` keeps reporting the claim zero for `calloc` (under
  `purged_is_zero`; under `lazy_free` it is treated as dirty, a cost, not
  an error); (3) the decay walks read the `empty` set only, so the pages
  stay resident until the refill; (4) `transfer_group`'s consistency check
  (`Purged` <=> in the donor's `purged` set) and `next_idle_group`'s
  cursor rule both hold: the slab is below the cursor (`next_slab` moved
  `group_next` past it) and in the set, so an idle-group scan sees it as
  idle and another arena may adopt it, which is the same memory serving
  the same class one arena over. Not chosen: the `empty` set with state
  `Fresh` (`purge_empty` asserts `Empty` for every member of that set and
  the walks would purge it); `Empty` (a purge would undo the prefault and
  `take` would report it dirty); a new state (touches `slab.rs`, the
  model, `is_zero` and every match on `State`, for a word that only
  changes the bins table's `purged` column). The one inaccuracy accepted:
  the `purged` count and the RSS derived from slab counts do not know the
  slab is resident until it is taken. A slab the adoption yields as
  `Empty` or `Purged` is put back into its set untouched and nothing is
  prefaulted (the refill takes it). The model op for lane C: a background
  step "if no Empty/Purged: commit next (as the refill would), mark
  Purged, add to purged set", with the invariant that a `Purged` slab
  reads zero unchanged.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane B, 2026-09-20), the populate happens under the
  class lock before the slab is published, not after: with the slab
  unpublished no refill can be writing into it, so the touch fallback is a
  plain write of zero over zero and `MADV_POPULATE_WRITE` never races a
  claim; the cost is one lock hold of a populate (a 64 KiB slab: one
  syscall installing 16 pages) on a class whose next refill would have
  taken those faults itself.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane B, 2026-09-20), the wake: `SlabTable::commit_slab`
  wakes the background thread once per usable fresh slab it commits (a
  relaxed load when the key is off; a `futex_wake` on the commit path,
  which just did an `mprotect`), so the pass runs right after the refill
  that consumed the previous prefaulted slab and a thread sleeping with no
  decay deadline still runs it; `note_refill` was not made to wake (one
  syscall per refill would not be free). The pass's own commits wake
  nobody (`IN_PASS`).
- Phase 2.5 task 16 (lane B, 2026-09-20), the prctl probe is the call and
  its undo, only on a process whose `PR_GET_THP_DISABLE` reads 0: a
  process whose parent set a THP policy (inherited across fork and exec)
  is reported `inherited` and left alone, and `thp_process:advised` then
  issues nothing (the parent's policy wins over a library's key). Under
  `advised` the probe's set/undo pair is followed by the real set: three
  `prctl`s at init, none later.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane B, 2026-09-20), the `slice_ext=` word is the
  kernel's answer, not the key's: `available` when the kernel has the
  option and `slice_ext:off` turned it off, `on` only when lock holders
  request it, and the probe's own reason (`kernel-disabled` on the 7.0
  GCE kernels, `no-slice_ctrl` before 7.0, `no-rseq`) otherwise; the same
  vocabulary as the `Slice extension:` line, checked against it by
  `tests/c/features.c` and `api::print`.
- Phase 2.5 task 16 (lane E, 2026-09-20), what the A/B says about the
  `thp` default on an `always` host, for the human's item 2 of the
  2026-09-20 decisions bullet (numbers, not a verdict): the task 16
  advice alone (`thp:never` vs `thp:auto`, same binary, same host) costs
  the Valkey model -10.4% (valkey1) / -8.1% (valkey1-free) and nothing on
  larson16, for no RSS change (584.9 MiB either way; the 32 MiB of huge
  pages the pre-task-16 tree took were the model's tables in challoc's
  huge region, 20 more PMD faults per run), and it is the whole
  +6.4 -> +11.7 / +4.2 -> +12.9 movement between the two cell days
  (against jemalloc here: `thp:auto` +6.3% / +1.1%, the default +16.1% /
  +9.0%). `thp:always` is parity with jemalloc (+3.2% / -3.1%, inside the
  spread) for +9.5% peak RSS on that heap (640 MiB, 636 MiB of
  AnonHugePages, 281 deferred splits per run) and +39.8% on larson16's
  retained memory (175 -> 245 MiB; jemalloc 270). `thp_process:advised`
  on top of the default is nil (+4.6% / -2.6% / -1.4%, the lists overlap
  over their whole range): the kernel takes the prctl (`PR_GET_THP_DISABLE`
  3, `thp_except_advised=yes`, the report line `host always process
  advised: PR_THP_DISABLE_EXCEPT_ADVISED in force`), `THP_enabled` in
  `/proc/<pid>/status` still reads 1 on 6.18, and with (a) already
  advising every challoc region it only removes the 2..4 MiB the program's
  own mappings took. A fourth option is visible in the pair: `never` on
  the class regions and the huge runs left eligible (the 32 MiB that moved
  are huge runs), lane B's file.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane A, 2026-09-20), the claim side reviewed against
  lane B's `Purged`-slab decision: confirmed, no claim-code change
  needed for correctness. `take` reports a prefaulted `Purged` slab zero
  under `purged_is_zero` (its pages are the kernel's fresh zero pages,
  populated or touched with a zero) and dirty under `lazy_free` (a
  `memset` in `calloc`, no error); `finish`'s `check_zero` covers the
  pick; the compaction claim (`pop_compact`, `claim_random`) goes
  through the same `plan`/`next_slab` and takes partial slabs first, so
  it only reaches a prefaulted slab when the class has no room, and it
  does not call `note_refill`, so a class in compact mode is never
  prefaulted (right: compaction consumes holes, not fresh slabs); the
  idle-group adoption moves a prefaulted slab as any `Purged` one; a
  sub-page slab group (`slabs_per_page > 1`, 64 KiB pages) can be
  discarded by `purge_group` with a prefaulted neighbour inside it, which
  undoes the prefault but keeps the memory zero (a cost, not an error).
  Two things the design cannot do without a descriptor bit, recorded
  rather than fixed: a truly purged (`MADV_DONTNEED`) slab and a
  prefaulted one are indistinguishable (`Purged`, `dirty_at` 0), so with
  both in the set `next_slab` takes the lower address, which may be the
  non-resident one; and `stats.purged` counts the resident prefaulted
  slabs as purged.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane A, 2026-09-20), both claim-side hooks kept,
  behind the same key (`prefault:true` with the background thread), on
  the burst probe's numbers (the results file, section 3): the wake from
  `plan` when a refill takes the arena's last idle slab of a class
  (`purge::prefault::note_taken`, counted as `taken`), and the pipeline
  sized to the class's refills since the last pass (`prefault_delta` in
  slots, `prefault_ahead` keeps `ceil(delta / nslots)` slabs idle, at
  least 1 and at most `MAX_AHEAD` = 8). The plan's "one slab per class"
  is the floor; a class taking several slabs per millisecond in Valkey's
  fill burst (350 MiB in a few seconds) got every other one from lane
  B's pass (main thread 95.3 k -> 59.5 k faults over the fill), the
  pipeline alone gives 24.0 k, the wake alone 59.5 k (with one slab per
  pass the pass is already running as fast as it can), both together
  16.5 k (-83% against the default). The cost is bounded RSS during a
  burst: at most 8 slabs per `(arena, class)` that refills at that rate
  (+0.6 MiB VmHWM on the probe). `next_slab` was split into its sets
  half and `next_fresh_slab` so the prefault step never takes one of the
  arena's own idle slabs out of the sets (the first form spun). The
  pipeline takes the class lock once per slab (c66048b): the populate
  runs under the lock while the slab is unpublished (lane B's decision),
  and eight populates in one hold is a hold a refill of the class on the
  main thread waits through; per slab the hold is lane B's.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane A, 2026-09-20), the burst probe as the hook
  attribution: `valkey-benchmark -t set -d 512 -r 500000 -n 5000000 -P 16
  -c 50` against a fresh server (the soak's first write run), the main
  thread's `minflt` over the run, three interleaved rounds per variant;
  the variants are quantum8 builds of the same tree with one constant
  changed (`MAX_AHEAD` 1; the `plan` wake compiled out), swapped in at
  the server's rpath. A finding on the way: the reference VM's Valkey
  build linked a quantum8 library from before lane B's commit (the soak
  script checks the build exists and never rebuilds it), so the first
  probe read no prefault at all; `make valkey STAGE=build` before any
  soak that follows a library change.
- Phase 2.5 task 15 (lane C): the grants test proves the mechanism per
  hold rather than over a window. Two things the 7.2 host showed, both in
  the test's comments: the kernel grants once per syscall-free stretch of a
  hold (a hold that polled `granted` every 13 us, the debug-build cost of
  1000 dependent multiply-adds, missed its grant and was then preempted
  ~350 more times without another; the poll is now 100 multiply-adds,
  ~1.3 us in debug), and an unlock that wakes a futex sleeper ends the
  grant before `released` can see it, so the test holds the thread's own
  lock. The restore is a test-only snapshot of three statics, not another
  `configure`, because `Unprobed` cannot be reached through the API. The
  test-only diagnostics (`nonvoluntary_ctxt_switches` per capped hold, the
  rounds-to-grant range) stay in the printed line: they are what tells
  "preempted but not granted" from "not preempted" when the skip line
  fires on some other kernel.
- Phase 2.5 task 8: the abort test's witness is the signal handler's
  `ucontext` program counter compared with the signature word, not a
  test-only looped section: it keeps proving the production sections'
  retry paths (the kernel moved the worker to their own `abort_ip`), and
  it costs no asm. The counter check is `rseq::aborts() >= 1`, not `>=
  in_section`: a signal that finds the worker already at an abort
  handler's entry (moved there by an earlier fixup, the increment not
  yet run) counts once more than the handler runs (716 against 712 in
  one `make test` on the C4), and the aarch64 increment is not atomic.
- Phase 2.5 acceptance (lane A, 2026-09-20): the fix the attribution supports
  is recorded as a design note, not implemented (results file section 8):
  (1) `usable_size` on a slab pointer answers from `Regions::classify` alone
  without `check_live` (the 09-18 aarch64 variant, rejected then because
  `free` had just loaded the same line; tasks 4e/4f removed that premise),
  which trades the abort of a use-after-free `malloc_usable_size` query for
  up to 117 cycles per SET on the C4, a human's decision like 4e; or (2)
  keep the check and put `bits_base[c]` beside `class_base[c]` in the dense
  `Regions` arrays so the word is three dependent loads away, like
  jemalloc's rtree. Measurement plan named (`valkey1-usable` under
  `quick-perf` on the C4, `quick` on both reference VMs and a C3 lab cell,
  then this soak protocol with the sidecar).
- Phase 2.5 defaults, the human's decisions (2026-09-20, on the
  coordinator's recommendations with the numbers in "Notes for the human"):
  (1) `thp`: the shipped `never` advises `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` on the class
  superblock and the metadata region only; the huge region stays eligible
  for the host's policy (option (d) of `2026-09-20-aarch64-cgos-thp-ab.md`:
  keeps the slab regions' RSS discipline and gives the huge tier back the
  32 MiB it took under `always` before task 16, expected to read as (b)
  `thp:auto` on Chainguard OS, +6.3% instead of +16.1% behind jemalloc on
  valkey1). Lane B implements, lane E re-measures on the cgos cell.
  (2) `percpu_arena`: `auto` stays thread caches; the mode remains the
  opt-in (its costs 4..38% on the rows, its RSS win with idle threads
  available through the key). (3) `prefault`: stays off; the README
  documents `prefault:true,background_thread:true` for Valkey-shaped
  services (write-phase main-thread faults -83%, p99 unmoved, +4..6 MiB
  per bursting heap). (4) the `malloc_usable_size` design note stays a
  note, not a task (a safety-versus-speed change: a use-after-free query
  would return a size instead of aborting). Phase 3 starts now; the
  hardware rows 2a (ii)/(vi) and 4b stay open until a host exists.
- Phase 2.5 task 16 (lane B, 2026-09-20): `never` is the slab regions' RSS
  discipline; the huge tier follows the host, as jemalloc's does. The
  class superblock and the metadata region are committed a few slabs or
  pages at a time, so a 2 MiB page under them is RSS the program never
  asked for (and the fork copy-on-write cost Redis and Valkey forbid),
  which is what `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` there prevents on an `always` host. A
  huge run is a whole allocation of a page or more the program asked for;
  jemalloc's `never` advises its slab extents and lets its large extents
  take what the host gives, and challoc did the same before task 16: the
  32 MiB of tables the Valkey model keeps in huge runs took 2 MiB pages
  and their loss was 10 points of `valkey1` on Chainguard OS for no RSS
  gain (585 MiB either way). So the huge reservation is skipped by kind,
  not recorded for `force_never` (a forced `never` behaves as a configured
  one), and the report line names what `never` covers so a reader on an
  `always` host is not surprised by `AnonHugePages` on the huge region.
  The alternatives and why not: `thp:always` as the default on `always`
  hosts buys parity with jemalloc for +9.5% RSS on the 585 MiB heap and
  +40% on larson's retained memory; a size-banded `always` adds a
  threshold with no row behind it; and `thp_process:advised` stays the
  key for "no huge page anywhere in the process", which `never` no longer
  claims for the huge region.

- `auto` never installs `wfe`, and the `wfe` path stays in the tree behind the key. Reason: it lost xmalloc-test by 3% with a quarter more RSS on both aarch64 CPUs and gained nothing on larson; unselected it costs nothing (one atomic load per slow-path entry, measured within noise), and a deployment that wants the power saving or a CPU with a different wake latency can turn it on and measure. The explicit `wfe` is refused without the event stream so a `wfe` no store ends is always bounded.
- The event stream is probed (`HWCAP_EVTSTRM`) but not added to the feature report line. Reason: a new key would change `tests/c/features.c` and every recorded line for a feature nothing acts on by default; `lock::spin_hint()` and `event_stream()` are what the tests check.
- The probe's suggestion lives in `Cpu::probe` (a side effect on `lock::SPIN_HINT`) rather than in `api/mod.rs`'s init. Reason: init and `features.rs` belong to other lanes; the suggestion is idempotent and a configured hint is kept against it.
- `Config::load` probes the CPU a second time only when `spin_hint` is set. Reason: init's probe result is not in reach from `config.rs`; the probe is under 50 us and only a set key needs its ISA.
- `src/api/mallctl.rs` got one match arm (`cstr_of` knows `auto|baseline|umwait|wfe`) so `opt.spin_hint` reads back a string: a file outside the lane's partition, flagged for the coordinator.
- The geometry derivation was removed entirely rather than left behind a key. Reason: the acceptance said "the code stays constant" on no improvement, and `tcache_nslots_small_max:512` already gives a deployment the RSS the smaller ladder buys.
- Form (a), an explicit-state checker, not `loom`. Reason: `loom` would need the real `ClassState` compiled against its atomics and locks (a `src/class.rs` change) and cannot express the pre-fix step order; the model is written against the real code's step order with the lines quoted, so a protocol change is a model change.
- Lock holds that touch only lock-protected state are one atomic step; `fetch_or`, `fetch_and`, `sub_live` and the memory write are one step each and interleave with anything, including another thread's lock hold. `add_live` and `claim_prefix` are folded into `take`'s step. Reason: those are the only lock-free steps of the protocol, and the folded reads have no contender the code relies on; stated in the module docs.
- `finish` waits for a clear bit instead of spinning `MAX_EMPTY_PASSES`; an unmet reservation therefore shows as a deadlock violation. Reason: a self-loop would not terminate the DFS; the accounting invariant already guarantees a bit exists.
- `Write(i)` past a short refill is a no-op. Reason: a program writes what it was given; with eight slots and three threads a `Refill(3)` can come up short, and that is the scenario, not a bug.
- The handoff is modelled as part of `Refill`'s locked step (as in `next_slab`), with `try_lock` skipping a held donor. Reason: that is where the real code does it, and it made the task 15 ownership gap reproducible.
- I added a Done line to plan task 13 as the unit asked, although the lane's file rule named task 5's text only; the coordinator may drop it.
- Section 18 is numbered after 17 and the source layout moved to 19, as asked. `src/sys/mod.rs:1` and `docs/plan.md:39` (Phase 0) still say "section 18" for the source layout; both are outside this lane's files, one-word fixes for the coordinator.
- The MTE default is `tag_check:async` as the plan says; the section states that async gives no faulting address (`SEGV_MTEAERR`), recommends `asymm` where `mte3=yes`, and makes `sync` the negative tests' mode. Reason: the plan's default is the cheap one; the address is a diagnostic the operator can switch on.
- Retag on free is the design (the plan's acceptance test "a read through a freed pointer faults" needs it); the variant that tags at pop only is written down for part 2 to price next to it, with what it gives up. Reason: the retag is `zero_on_free`'s line fills, the pop-only sweep touches lines the program writes anyway; only measurement decides, and the human decides after it.
- The cache holds tagged pointers, so the pop is untouched and the tag work sits on `free`, the refill and the pop of a zero-prefix slot. Reason: the pop is the four-instruction path.
- The software tag is six bits at 57 on both architectures (LAM_U57's bits) and `lam` on aarch64 means TBI. Reason: one encoding, one mask table; TBI makes the whole software-tag half of part 2 runnable on the reference C4A now.
- The tag map is one byte per slot (relaxed load/store, no RMW) rather than nibbles. Reason: two owners of one slot is the double free the compare catches; a nibble would need an RMW on a byte two slots share.
- The sized path verifies the tag at the call under the key (one byte load and store), giving up 6.3's "no metadata load per sized free" only when the key is on. Reason: the key exists to check pointers; the bit test stays deferred.
- `challoc_features()` does not gain a `tagging=` key; the effective mode is a `malloc_stats_print` line with the reason. Reason: the line is built in init step 1 before the configuration is parsed and reports hardware, not decisions.
- No `src/` change and no results file: the task is design, and the task 4 matrix already holds the probe results per CPU (the plan's acceptance names the matrix file).
- (e) as a shell test (tests/as-needed.sh, `make check-needed`), not a test in
  src/api/ctests.rs: the lane never edits src/. The test's `none-default` case
  is informational (records the toolchain default) since it depends on the gcc
  spec, and the two-level case asserts the glibc binding it found.
- (d) the soak's results go to RESULT_DIR under `.tmp-out/` on the VM (rsync
  --delete on sync would remove bench/results/ files written on the VM) and are
  copied into bench/results/ by hand with the `-iothreads4` name; the summary is
  the soak script's own table with a reference column added by hand.
- (c) allocator stats via fiddle -> mallctl (`mallctl`, then `je_mallctl`) and a
  malloc_stats_print dump to stderr at the end of each run, kept beside the CSV
  as `-mstats.txt`; jemalloc's 65-arena report trimmed to general + merged (+
  arenas with threads) before committing. `challoc-1arena` (MALLOC_CONF=
  narenas:1) added as an optional variant after the x86_64 run showed 17
  per-thread arenas; run on aarch64 only (one extra 10-minute run).
- Both soak scripts take COMMIT: a worktree synced to a VM has a `.git` file
  pointing at a Mac path, so git in the container says "unknown".
- The Makefile hunks were staged per prerequisite; the .PHONY line and the two
  help blocks (they name both ruby-soak and check-needed) went with (e).
- No limit is eager. `MADV_FREE` pages stay charged and in RSS until reclaim and would mislead Valkey's RSS fragmentation ratio and autoscalers (design 8 said so about `lazy_free`); the lazy advice is a saving the policy grants only where it knows the headroom (`Low` band). Recorded in design 8 and the design 11 table's new advice column.
- Sticky zero flag instead of a per-slab advice bit: `purged_is_zero()` turns false at the first lazy batch and stays false; `calloc` then clears `Purged` memory it reuses (a `memset` on mostly-resident pages), `Fresh` stays known zero, `malloc` unaffected. Chosen because the per-slab form needs a descriptor bit read in `class::take`/`huge::take` (not lane B's files) for a saving bounded to `calloc` on `Purged` reuse; the protocol rows do not move. Recorded in design 8.
- Explicit purges are eager whatever the band (`purge_all`/`purge_arena` hold an `Explicit` guard): `malloc_trim(0)` and `arena.<i>.purge` mean "give RSS back now", and the `high`-rise full purge runs because the kernel is reclaiming. `lazy_free:true` still wins there (decay.c `lazy` mode unchanged).
- Advice keys live under `stats.cgroup.*` (the plan's wording) but read without a tracked cgroup, unlike their siblings: the advice exists either way (eager, or lazy under `lazy_free`).
- The new key is `lazy_band` (bool) rather than a tri-state `lazy_free`: `opt.lazy_free` stays a bool for jemalloc-shaped readers.
- `auto` refuses where the PMD size is `madvise`-gated (`hugepages-2048kB/enabled` `madvise`, or `inherit` under a `madvise` top level, Ubuntu's default). The same `MADV_HUGEPAGE` that enables the 64 KiB folio would make the region eligible for 2 MiB pages at a fault over an untouched aligned range and, slowly, through khugepaged (`max_ptes_none` 511 collapses any range with one present page), and `auto`'s promise is no 2 MiB page. The host requirement is therefore `hugepages-64kB/enabled=madvise` (or `always`) with `hugepages-2048kB/enabled=never` (or `always`, the host's own choice for every process); recorded in design 17 and the results file. On Ubuntu's default sysfs `auto` is inert and the report says why.
- `always` commits and advises whole PMD-sized chunks rather than advising per slab: the VMA grows one slab at a time, so a per-slab advice would give 2 MiB pages only through khugepaged (16 MiB per 10 s), invisible in a two-minute row and unlike jemalloc's 2 MiB extents; the chunk makes the first touch a 2 MiB fault. Every slab of a chunk re-issues the two calls (a microsecond each, no-ops after the first), since groups are 1..16 slabs and arenas commit theirs in any order; the counters take the call that starts the chunk.
