- Guard slabs inside a chunk are left unused and unguarded under `always`: a guard marker (or a `PROT_NONE` page) inside a PMD-mapped page splits it, which the first form did to every chunk. The trade is recorded in design 5 and 17; `auto` keeps the guards (its folio is the slab itself).
- Metadata follows `thp` (no separate `metadata_thp`, which stays an accepted jemalloc key): tables are folio-aligned at reservation and their commits rounded to the folio, so the first touch maps one folio; never a 2 MiB page for the metadata (a table's first page would cost 2 MiB of RSS). The review's `MADV_COLLAPSE`-for-hot-metadata idea is therefore probe-only: the advice at commit makes a copy afterwards unnecessary, and the syscall cannot collapse below the PMD through 7.0.
- The `MADV_COLLAPSE` probe runs only when `thp` is not `never` and the line says `collapse=unprobed` by default: populating and collapsing 2 MiB in every process's init is not a cost the default configuration should pay for a reported-only fact.
- `auto` is not forced to `never` under a cgroup limit below 1 GiB (design 11's rule stays for `always`): a 64 KiB folio is the slab's own granularity and the metadata rounding is bounded by the tables in use (+0.1% RSS on the 500 MiB heap, +2 MiB on a 16 MiB one).
- Default `never`, `auto` an opt-in: the plan's third condition (scorecard-shaped rows do not lose) fails on larson (1.6..1.7%, disjoint lists) and on small-heap RSS, while the target rows are flat; the numbers are in front of the human in the results file for a later reversal on aarch64 hosts that enable the knob.
- Phase 3 task 1: the runtime package `challoc` ships only `/usr/lib/libchalloc.so.0`; the `libchalloc.so` link name goes to `challoc-dev` through Wolfi's `split/dev`. Reason: design 13.6 calls the link "for the linker", and every Wolfi library package splits it that way (`jemalloc.yaml`'s `libjemalloc2`/`jemalloc-dev`); the `challoc` test asserts the link is absent from the runtime.
- Phase 3 task 1: `challoc-dev` carries a `challoc.pc` written by the pipeline (the build has none): `Version` is the crate version and `Libs` is `-L${libdir} -Wl,--push-state,--no-as-needed -lchalloc -Wl,--pop-state`. Reason: design 13.6 (a) says a package must link the executable with `--no-as-needed` for the allocator library or the two-level shape silently runs on glibc malloc; putting the scoped flag in the pc file makes `$(pkg-config --libs challoc)` correct by default, and the package test proves it both ways (a program referencing nothing keeps `DT_NEEDED libchalloc.so.0` with the pc flags, loses it with a plain `-lchalloc`). `jemalloc.pc` is left exactly as `scripts/jemalloc-compat.sh` writes it (`-ljemalloc`), since jemalloc consumers such as Ruby already add `--no-as-needed` themselves and the file must match upstream's `jemalloc.pc.in`.
- Phase 3 task 1: `challoc-jemalloc-compat` declares `provides: libjemalloc2=${{package.full-version}}` and no `provider-priority`. Reason (measured in clean containers with both repositories visible): apk prefers the package that carries a name over a provider, so `apk add ruby-3.4` still installs Wolfi's `libjemalloc2` and nothing flips merely because the compat package exists; `apk add challoc-jemalloc-compat` on top purges `libjemalloc2` and Ruby runs on challoc, and naming both together picks the compat library directly, which is how a task 3 consumer swaps (name it in `dependencies.runtime` and in place of `jemalloc-dev` in its environment). A `provider-priority` above `libjemalloc2`'s (none) would switch every jemalloc consumer at once; below it is impossible. `jemalloc-dev` is not provided: the compat package has no header, and apk already refuses to co-install the two (`pc:jemalloc` conflict).
- Phase 3 task 1: the source step is `git-checkout` of `github.com/chainguard-dev/challoc` at `v${{package.version}}`, guarded by a melange build option `local-source` that skips it and builds the `--source-dir` tree. Reason: Wolfi convention needs a tagged upstream, this repository has neither a public remote nor a tag yet, and the option lets the same file be built from a working tree without editing it; `expected-commit` is the `main` commit the file was written against (4a7d30c), to be replaced by the tag's commit in the Wolfi PR. A version-check step fails the build if `Cargo.toml` or `VERSION_STR` moves without the YAML.
- Phase 3 task 1: no `libchalloc.a` in any package. Reason: the Phase 1 task 6 decision (no static compat library until a `-static` link's fork and TLS behaviour is tested) applies to the system package as well; `split/static` is a one-line addition when it is.
- Phase 3 task 1: the compat build runs `scripts/jemalloc-compat.sh` inside the pipeline instead of restating its steps. Reason: the staged file set and `jemalloc.pc` are then byte-for-byte what `make jemalloc-compat` and the `api::ctests::jemalloc_compat_stage` test check, and a change to the script reaches the package without a second edit.
- Phase 3 task 2: the private `quantum8` library is built inside `valkey-9.1.yaml`, not shipped as a `challoc-valkey` subpackage of `challoc.yaml` nor as a `challoc-valkey.yaml`. Reason: melange's SCA (`pkg/sca/sca.go`) lists a shared object's soname under `provides:` only when the file is directly in `lib/`, `usr/lib/`, `lib64/`, `usr/lib64/` (plus `ld.so.conf.d` paths), otherwise under `vendored:`, and `removeSelfProvidedDeps` (`pkg/build/package.go`) prunes a package's own `so:` dependency only against its own vendored list; built in the Valkey package, `/usr/lib/valkey/libchalloc.so.0` is `vendored: so:libchalloc.so.0=0`, `valkey-server`'s `DT_NEEDED` dependency is pruned, and `valkey-9.1` neither provides the soname (nothing else can pick the quantum8 build) nor depends on any challoc package; as a separate package, `valkey-9.1` would keep an automatic `so:libchalloc.so.0` dependency that apk satisfies with the system `challoc`, a second unused copy in every Valkey image removable only with `no-depends`. It is also design 13.6's wording ("a private quantum8 build of the same source in its own package, the way it gets jemalloc today") and Wolfi's shape for a library a consumer must carry privately (built in the consumer's pipeline, its source in the SBOM through the `git-checkout`, rebuilt by an epoch bump). Cost: one 8 s `cargo build` and `rust-1.98` in every Valkey stream's environment.
- Phase 3 task 2: the client tools keep their allocator link. Valkey links every binary with `FINAL_LIBS`, so `valkey-cli` and `valkey-benchmark` carry `DT_NEEDED libchalloc.so.0` and the same rpath, and the `-cli`/`-benchmark` subpackages get an automatic `so:libchalloc.so.0` dependency satisfied by the system `challoc` (measured: `apk add valkey-9.1-cli` installs `challoc-0.1.0-r0`); next to `valkey-9.1` the rpath makes them load the private copy (`LD_DEBUG=libs`). Reason: correct for them (no quantum requirement; `valkey-cli --version` runs with the system library preloaded), and the alternatives are worse: `valkey-cli` cannot be built without the allocator its `zmalloc.c` calls (`USE_JEMALLOC` is per tree, not per binary), and a static link from `libchalloc.a` is untested (`contrib/wolfi/README.md`, "Nothing is shipped from libchalloc.a"). The test asserts the two entries on all three binaries; the earlier `! readelf | grep` negation was a no-op under `set -e` and is gone.
- Phase 3 task 2: `-Wl,-rpath,/usr/lib/valkey` produces `DT_RPATH` with Wolfi's binutils 2.47 (no `--enable-new-dtags` default); design 13.6 says "RUNPATH". Kept as the linker gives it, the definition and test accept `RUNPATH|RPATH`. Reason: for a private library `DT_RPATH` is the better semantics (searched before `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, so an environment pointing at `/usr/lib` cannot swap in the quantum16 build; only `LD_PRELOAD` can, and then the server dies at start instead of running on the wrong class table), and it is one flag (`-Wl,--enable-new-dtags`) if Wolfi wants `RUNPATH`.
- Phase 3 task 2: the package test starts the server on a unix socket (`port 0`, `unixsocket /tmp/valkey.sock`) rather than a TCP port. Reason: the task's "free port" is guaranteed by construction and `valkey-cli -s`/`valkey-benchmark -s` cover everything the test needs; the by-hand checks used TCP 6399 to show the same on a port.
- Phase 3 task 2: the pipeline's check step runs the freshly built `valkey-server` with `LD_LIBRARY_PATH=<destdir>/usr/lib/valkey`. Reason: the rpath does not exist in the build environment, and the loader wants the soname file name (`libchalloc.so.0`, which only the staged copy has; cargo's output is `libchalloc.so`). Two build failures before this was right; both in the results file.
- Phase 3 task 5 (c): the load generator is `ab` from Wolfi's apache2-utils
  because Wolfi has neither `wrk` nor `oha` (apk search, 2026-09-20). ab is
  single-threaded and one URL per process, so the server row runs two ab
  processes at once (32 keep-alive connections each, /json and /blob) against
  one node process; `-t DURATION` must precede `-n` because ab's -t resets
  -n to 50000, and `-l` accepts the variable-length JSON body. Node's single
  event loop shared by both endpoints makes the two req/s figures equal (the 1
  MiB /blob responses set the pace); the row is still a same-generator
  comparison across allocators.
- Phase 3 task 5 (c): the server keeps an explicit Content-Length on the 1 MiB
  body (written as 64 KiB subarray views of one Buffer.allocUnsafe(1 << 20)),
  so ab sees a fixed-length keep-alive response rather than chunked encoding;
  the backing store is still one malloc(1 MiB) per request, freed when the GC
  collects the Buffer, which is what the row is for.
- Phase 3 task 5 (c): server RSS is the process's smaps_rollup Rss sampled every
  5 s (peak and final) plus VmHWM at the end, and the end-of-load smaps summed
  by mapping name (challoc:classes|huge|meta) so an RSS gap is placed without a
  second run; the process rows use bench/rt's maxrss as the Ruby and Python
  rows do.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the load generator is `ab` (Wolfi's `apache2-utils`);
  `wrk` and `oha` are not in Wolfi (checked 2026-09-20). Two `ab -k -r -l
  -c 32` processes run concurrently, one per endpoint, for the 64
  connections and "both endpoints" of the plan; `-n` after `-t` because
  `-t` alone caps ab at 50000 requests. Each ab runs under `bench/rt` so
  its CPU share is on record (63..87% of a core in the runs: not saturated).
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): `-Dsun.net.httpserver.nodelay=true` in the default
  `JAVA_OPTS`. The JDK server leaves TCP_NODELAY off and writes headers
  and body separately, so the small JSON response waits for the client's
  delayed ACK: 775 req/s at p99 44 ms against 76k req/s on /blob in the
  Mac container without it. Without the flag the JSON row measures the
  ACK timer, not the allocator.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): `-XX:+AlwaysPreTouch` beside the plan's fixed
  `-Xms/-Xmx`, so the Java heap is resident from the start under every
  allocator and the RSS columns differ by native memory only;
  `-XX:MaxDirectMemorySize` left at its default (= `-Xmx`), so the direct
  buffers churn up to 512 MiB before `Bits.reserveMemory` forces a GC.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the server's CPU per request (`/proc/<pid>/stat`
  over the measured window divided by the requests both generators
  completed) is a column beside req/s: it is the allocator's cost per unit
  of work whether or not the closed-loop generator sets the rate, and it
  is what separated "3% fewer requests" from "3% more CPU" on x86_64.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): challoc's `MALLOC_CONF=stats_print:true` is inert
  (`src/config.rs` INERT_KEYS), so the server row preloads
  `bench/probes/statsdump.so` after the allocator: an `atexit` hook that
  calls `malloc_stats_print` through `dlsym` where the allocator exports
  it (challoc, jemalloc) and does nothing elsewhere. Harness only, no
  `src/` change (lane J changes nothing under `src/`).
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the plan's "idle RSS" is the server's Rss 10 s after
  it answered, before the load (the `idle Rss` column), beside `java
  -version`'s max RSS; the 5-minute rows ran in full, no shortening.
- Phase 3 task 3 (coordinator's decision, on the lane's recommendation; recorded for the human to reverse): the compat header ships as a new subpackage `challoc-jemalloc-compat-dev` with `/usr/include/jemalloc/jemalloc.h` and `provides: jemalloc-dev=${{package.full-version}}` (no `provider-priority`). Reason: Ruby's `--with-jemalloc` probes `<jemalloc/jemalloc.h>` and reads `malloc_conf` through it, and `jemalloc-dev` conflicts with the compat library on the link name and the pc file; a header package of `jemalloc-dev`'s file set makes the swap a one-name change in the consumer's build environment. Measured: `apk add jemalloc-dev` while it is installed is a no-op (the name is provided; task 1's refusal is gone), `apk add ruby-3.4` from Wolfi's repository alone still installs Wolfi's jemalloc. Reversible by dropping the `provides:` line (then the two -dev packages conflict on files and apk refuses, as in task 1).
- Phase 3 task 3: the link name `/usr/lib/libjemalloc.so` and `jemalloc.pc` moved from `challoc-jemalloc-compat` (task 1) to the -dev package. Reason: `split/dev`'s convention and Wolfi's own split (`libjemalloc2` = the `.so.2`, `jemalloc-dev` = link, pc, headers), so each compat package mirrors one Wolfi package exactly and `provides:` names the right one; the task 1 tests moved with the files.
- Phase 3 task 3: the header declares no standard entry point (`malloc`, `free`, ..., `malloc_usable_size`) and includes `<stdlib.h>` and `<malloc.h>` instead; the `je_` names are prototypes, not rename macros. Reason: jemalloc's header redeclares them with its own attributes and `JEMALLOC_USABLE_SIZE_CONST` juggling to stay compatible with glibc's `malloc.h`, which Ruby includes into every translation unit through `ruby/missing.h` (C++ extensions included); glibc's declarations are the ones that must win, and both spellings are real exported symbols of the library, so no macro is needed and `<challoc/jemalloc.h>` can be included alongside.
- Phase 3 task 3: `ruby-3.4`'s `dependencies.runtime` names `challoc-jemalloc-compat` explicitly. Reason: melange derives `so:libjemalloc.so.2` from the binaries and apk resolves a bare `so:` name to the package that carries it (`libjemalloc2`) over a provider (task 1 measurement), so without the name an image would run the package on Wolfi's jemalloc while its test ran on challoc.
- Phase 3 task 3: the package test proves the allocator from inside the interpreter through Fiddle (`mallctl("version")`, `challoc_version()`, `malloc` identity, `malloc_stats_print`) rather than with `MALLOC_CONF=stats_print:true`. Reason: challoc does not implement `stats_print` (accepted, inert, `src/config.rs` `INERT_KEYS`), so the prompt's assertion would have relied on the diagnostic for an inert key; that line is kept in a form that also passes once the key is implemented.
- Phase 3 task 3 (coordinator's decision (2), recorded for the human to reverse): design 13.6's "RUNPATH" wording is now "rpath (`DT_RPATH` or `DT_RUNPATH`)" with the finding that Wolfi's binutils 2.47 emits `DT_RPATH` for `-Wl,-rpath`, searched before `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, which for Valkey's private library is the better of the two. The alternative is `-Wl,--enable-new-dtags` in `valkey-9.1.yaml`.
- Phase 3 task 4: the key table is in `KEYS` order, not grouped by topic, so a reader can diff it against `config.rs` and the `opt.` block of the report line by line; the topical reading is in the sections after it (consumers, cgroup, THP). One row per key with six columns; the reasons for the defaults are a list after the table rather than a seventh column, so the rows stay one line.
- Phase 3 task 4: every number in the guide is one read from a `bench/results/` file this iteration, and the file is named next to it. Where the README or design rounded a figure differently from the results file, the guide carries the file's figure: the `double_free_scan` cost is given as the x86_64 valkey1 medians (14.828 -> 13.004 M ops/s, `2026-09-18-x86_64-gce-detection-gaps.md`) rather than the README's "up to 14%"; the `thp:auto` small-heap cost as larson1's 15.9 -> 18.1 MiB (`2026-09-19-lane-b-mthp.md`) rather than design 17's "7..19%"; the two-advice `pglazyfree` rise as 188 MiB (the file) rather than design 8's 189.
- Phase 3 task 4: the Ruby section says "nothing to set" as the task asked and records the `narenas:1` soak finding as a packaging option not taken, with the caveat that one arena's contention was not measured; it does not recommend it. The JVM and Node sections record the rows and name the one knob each row points at (`narenas` as the `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX` analogue, `huge_retain_max` for Node's 1 MiB buffers) without recommending a value, since no lower value was measured.
- Phase 3 task 4: the report-reading section quotes the exact strings the code prints (the `Prefault:` forms including the `taken N` tail, the `Per-CPU caches:` probe words, the six `purge advice:` reasons, the `thp:` reason texts) rather than paraphrasing design 16, so an operator can grep a report for them.
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): the server runs as a non-root OS user (`pgb`) the script creates with busybox `adduser`, started through busybox `su -s /bin/sh pgb -c "env LD_PRELOAD=... pg_ctl start"`. Reason: postgres refuses to run as root and Wolfi's busybox `setpriv` has no `--reuid`; `env LD_PRELOAD` inside the command line sets the environment after the uid change, so the postmaster and every process it forks inherit it (checked in the postmaster's and one backend's `/proc/<pid>/maps`) and nothing setuid is involved.
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): the RSS sampler runs as the postgres user, not as the container's root. Reason: measured on 2026-09-20, root inside the container gets EACCES on another uid's `/proc/<pid>/maps` and `smaps_rollup` (no `CAP_SYS_PTRACE`); the processes' owner can read them, so `su pgb -c 'sh bench/probes/pg-rss-sampler.sh ...'` needs no `--cap-add` in the Makefile.
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): RSS is reported both as the sum of `Pss` (the plan's measure: every shared_buffers page counted once across the postmaster and its backends) and as the sum of `Pss_Anon`, plus the per-backend medians of both. Reason: with `shared_buffers=1GB` the Pss sum is dominated by shared memory identical for every allocator; `Pss_Anon` (heaps and stacks) is where the allocators differ, and a percentage needs both.
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): "final" RSS is the last sampler pass with at least 90% of the run's maximum backend count, and the table shows the passes per run. Reason: a pass over 500 backends' `smaps_rollup` takes tens of seconds on a saturated host (3 passes per 60 s run at 500 backends, about 6 at 100), so the last pass could straddle the backends' exit and count 200-300 of 500; the first render did, and `REPORT_ONLY=1` re-rendered both files from the raw samples (the header says so and keeps the run's commit, date and duration).
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): one data set (`pgbench -i -s 50`, loaded once under glibc) shared by every allocator, one server start per allocator per round, `shared_buffers=1GB`, `synchronous_commit=off`, no checkpoint inside a row, `huge_pages=off`. Reason: the data files do not depend on the server's allocator; the scale-50 set (810 MB) fits shared memory so the select rows are CPU-bound in the backends rather than in read() and the page cache; synchronous_commit=off keeps TPC-B off the hyperdisk's fsync latency; huge pages off so the shared segment is mapped the same on both VMs.
- Phase 3 task 5 (b): p99 comes from `pgbench -l --sampling-rate=0.05` (a 5% sample of the transaction latencies, one log per pgbench thread, sorted after the run and deleted). Reason: pgbench prints no percentile, `-r` gives per-statement averages only, a full log at 300k+ tps is gigabytes over the protocol; a 5% sample is 300k+ latencies per run.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the server row's JVMs run with
  `-XX:NativeMemoryTracking=summary` and the harness takes `jcmd <pid>
  VM.native_memory summary scale=KB` at the peak-Rss sample (whenever a
  sample sets a new peak, at most once per 30 s, so the snapshot on record
  is the one at the highest Rss the run reached) and at the end; the
  server table carries NMT committed and "allocator overhead" = Rss minus
  NMT committed at the same instant, and a separate table has the
  categories (Java Heap, Class, Thread, Code, GC, Compiler, Internal,
  Other, the malloc total, NMT's own overhead). NMT costs a 16-byte header
  per malloc plus its tables, the same bytes for every allocator, so the
  comparisons hold; the header does turn the row's `malloc(65536)` into
  `malloc(65552)`, one class up (80 KiB) in challoc (`src/layout.rs`:
  four classes per doubling up to `MEDIUM_MAX`), jemalloc and mimalloc,
  so the NMT'd server row is compared with the NMT-less first iteration
  (469d72d) in the results files' reading rather than treated as the same
  measurement.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the seventh variant `glibc-trim` is glibc with the
  JVM's own `-XX:+UnlockExperimentalVMOptions
  -XX:TrimNativeHeapInterval=5000` (a `malloc_trim(0)` from the JVM's
  service thread every 5 s): Wolfi's openjdk-21 21.0.12.1 accepts the
  flag (`java -XX:+UnlockExperimentalVMOptions
  -XX:TrimNativeHeapInterval=5000 -version` exits 0, with or without the
  Unlock flag: it is a product flag in this 21u), so no `jcmd
  System.trim_native_heap` sidecar was needed; the script checks the flag
  at start and refuses the variant if a JDK rejects it, rather than
  silently measuring plain glibc.
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the soak is a mode of `HttpBench` (`soak PORT CORE
  MAX KEEPALIVE_S MIN_BYTES MAX_BYTES`), not a second program: the same
  endpoints and reply path, a `ThreadPoolExecutor(16, 400, 2 s,
  SynchronousQueue, CallerRunsPolicy)` so a request beyond the live
  threads starts a thread and idle threads retire after 2 s, and a
  `MixedBlobHandler` drawing the body size log-uniform in [256, 1 MiB]
  per request (`exp(ln min + u (ln max - ln min))`), one
  `ByteBuffer.allocateDirect(n)` per request, copied out to the socket in
  64 KiB heap chunks so the Java heap sees no humongous arrays and the
  native side is what varies. The generator is two `ab -k` processes per
  60 s window (json and blob, half the connections each: `-c 128` x2 in
  the high windows, `-c 4` x2 in the low), 20 windows for the 1200 s;
  req/s, p99 and the server's CPU per request are per window, Rss and
  threads at each window's end, `/stats` gives the pool's live and
  largest size. The slope is the least-squares fit of Rss over the
  samples of the last 600 s in MiB per minute; the series CSVs
  (`<result>-<allocator>.csv`, `-windows.csv`) are copied beside the
  results file. `-XX:MaxDirectMemorySize` stays at its default (= -Xmx
  512m), so up to 512 MiB of direct buffers are live before
  `Bits.reserveMemory` forces a GC: the soak's Rss sits around 1.0..1.4
  GiB under every allocator (2.1 under aarch64's 64 KiB-page jemalloc)
  and the allocator's share is what differs. The soak's raw output has
  its own OUT (`target/jvm/soak-out`, 585e20b) because every run starts
  with `rm -rf OUT` and the server row's re-run erased the amd64 soak's
  stats print before it was read (the aarch64 print is complete and is
  the one quoted; the amd64 file says so).
- Phase 3 task 5 (a): the pool did not reach 256 threads (largest 72..122,
  peak thread count 68..157) because a single `ab` process at `-c 128` on
  the log-uniform bodies is CPU-bound (93..99% of a core, 4.2..5.3 GiB/s);
  the pool still cycled 16 -> ~100 -> 16 every two minutes for the whole
  run, which is the thread churn the soak wanted. Kept as is and stated
  in the files; a multi-process generator would raise the ceiling.

## Notes for the human

- Phase 2.5 task 7 follow-up, for the human (lane A, 2026-09-20): the
  Altra `SIGILL` was a shipped-library bug, not only a test bug: since task
  7 landed (9157450) every aarch64 flush executed `xpacd` through
  `pac::mask()` whatever the probe said, so challoc on a Neoverse N1 host
  (Altra, Graviton2) died at its first cache flush; the four-way and
  CPU-matrix runs on T2A predate task 7 (7b2c50b). Fixed in b43532f, proven
  on the Mac by counting PAC instructions in a forked child with the probe
  forced off (0 executed); the T2A itself has not run since (lane D's cell,
  `make test` there closes the ceiling gap (i)). No T2A VM was created here
  (the forced-off proof stood, as the brief allowed).
- Phase 2.5 task 8 follow-up, for the human (lane A, 2026-09-20): the mode
  is cheaper by about half but not free, and the default stays thread
  caches. Where it stands (`ab`, C4A / C4, against dfe3f60's default):
  larson1 +5.3% / +3.7%, larson10 +9.4% / +16.6%, xmalloc +18.9% / +16.1%,
  valkey1 +4.7% / +37.8%, alloc-testN +4.8% / +3.9%. What is attributed:
  the per-side section floor (+47 / +33 instructions per larson operation,
  4 cycles: the rseq descriptor store, the `mm_cid` load and range check,
  the table index, the status word, the 7-instruction byte count), and
  the Valkey model's remaining +28 / +31 cycles (the scheduled pop's cold
  path once in 8 pops and one block claim per `cap / 4` of them; `quick`'s
  5 s score reads the write phase only, where compaction is busiest, hence
  +39% there against +22% in cycles per operation). What is not attributed
  and would be next: the larson10 and xmalloc rows (cross-thread frees and
  a producer/consumer): the per-CPU flush copies the stack out and back
  through two sections and releases by owner, where the thread flush works
  in place and `free_other` batches per arena, and xmalloc's RSS under the
  mode (53 -> 82 MiB on the C4A) says the copies keep more slots in
  flight; a `quick-perf` with the sampler split by function on xmalloc is
  the first step. Two cheap trims of the floor if you want them: the byte
  count as a per-heap per-class counter (4 instructions instead of 7 per
  side; `thread.allocated` summed at stats time) and a `pop_raw` variant
  without the `zeroed` compare for `malloc` (3..5 instructions per pop,
  a second asm block per architecture). If the default is ever flipped,
  the change is in `heap::Percpu::build` (lane A's file): delete the `Auto`
  arm of its match so `auto` follows the probe as the key's doc in
  `config.rs` already says; nothing in `config.rs` needs to move.
- Phase 2.5 task 11 (lane A, 2026-09-20), the plan, not done: prefaulted
  fresh slabs need the background thread (lane B's `purge.rs`) and the
  commit path (lane B's `class.rs` half), so nothing was coded. The
  claim-side hook it needs already exists: `ClassState::refilled` counts
  refills per (arena, class) and `note_refill` is called on every per-CPU
  and thread refill, so a background pass can find "classes that refilled
  since the last pass" by diffing that counter against a snapshot of its
  own; the pass would then commit the class's next fresh slab (the commit
  path's `commit next` step, but from the thread and without a claim) and
  `MADV_POPULATE_WRITE` it (5.14+, probed in `features.rs`; fallback:
  touch one byte per page), one slab per class per pass, bounded by the
  cgroup headroom (`policy.rs`'s band), and the claim path would take the
  prefaulted slab as it takes any committed empty slab (lowest-address
  selection already prefers it once committed). Acceptance as the plan
  says: the Valkey soak's write-phase p99 and the main thread's page
  faults from the perf sidecar, scorecard within noise. Suggested owner:
  lane B, with lane A reviewing the claim side.
- Phase 2.5 task 8, for the human (lane A, 2026-09-19): the per-CPU mode
  is implemented, tested both ways and measured, and it loses to thread
  caches on every `quick` row on both reference VMs (the Design decision
  above has the numbers; the `ab` tables are in
  `bench/results/2026-09-19-{aarch64,x86_64}-task8-percpu.md`), so it ships
  as an opt-in (`percpu_arena:percpu`). Two things are yours: (1) the key's
  default in `src/config.rs` (lane C's file, untouched here) still reads
  `Auto` and its doc says `auto` follows the probe; `heap::Percpu::build`
  reads `Auto` as "thread caches for now". If the opt-in stands, the
  one-line change is `percpu_arena: PercpuArena::Off` in `Config::DEFAULT`
  (plus `percpu_arena_values`'s default assertion and the section 12 row),
  and `OffAuto` can then mean what `enabled()` says; if you want it on,
  delete the `Auto` arm of `Percpu::build`'s match. (2) Where the cost is:
  the mode's pop is the thread-pointer load, the `mm_cid` load, the table
  index and a critical section in a called function, against the thread
  pop's two loads and a store inline in `malloc`; the fast-tier rows are
  the price of that per operation (10..24%), and the x86_64 Valkey model's
  +67% is the compact-mode scheduled pop claiming one slot under the class
  lock per 8 pops on a fragmented 500 MiB heap (the thread run amortises
  one lock over `cap / 4`); a per-CPU run would need a two-word commit the
  design has no single store for. The RSS the mode buys is only visible
  with more threads than cores (the Ruby idle-thread rows in the results
  files).
- Phase 2.5 task 8 (lane A, 2026-09-19), rseq offsets and sizes observed
  under this task, all read from the symbols at run time (never
  constants): on both reference VMs (kernel 7.0.0-1011-gcp, glibc 2.44)
  `__rseq_size=33`, `__rseq_flags=0`; `__rseq_offset=320` (aarch64) and
  `-384` (x86_64) inside the `cargo test` binaries, against 192 / -192
  bare and -256 under the preload measured for the prerequisites, which is
  the static-TLS dependence the design warns about; `mm_cid` reads 16
  distinct values over 16 busy threads on each VM (`nr_cpu_ids` 16). On
  the Mac's Docker VM `__rseq_size=20` (`__rseq_offset=320`), the mode is
  off with verdict `no-mm_cid` and every per-CPU test takes its fallback
  branch.
- Phase 2.5 task 7, for the human (2026-09-19): `pac_pointers` is off by default by the plan's own fallback; the numbers to weigh (`bench/results/2026-09-19-lane-a-pac-pointers.md`): with the key on the C4A pays xmalloc +11.0%, alloc-test1 +3.0%, valkey1 +2.0%, larson1 +1.2%, larson10 +1.9% (`quick`), and per operation larson1 +1.0 cycle, valkey1 +13.0, valkey1-free +16.2 (`quick-perf`); signing the cache entries only (heap pointer plain, a measurement build, not shipped) halves the Valkey cost (+7.4 per SET) and xmalloc's (+5.5%). The floor is two PAC instructions per cached entry and one `autda` per call; nothing cheaper is available without dropping a guarantee. If you want a default on Axion, the split key (entries only) is the trade to consider, and it still fails the 5% gate on xmalloc alone. FEAT_FPAC is present on both the Mac's Apple silicon and Axion, so a corrupted entry is a `SIGILL` at the `autda` in `malloc` on every PAC host we have; the non-FPAC path (Neoverse N1, Apple M1: non-canonical pointer, `SIGSEGV` on first use) is untested on hardware.
- Phase 2.5 task 8 prerequisites, gathered under this iteration (`.tmp-rseq.c` under the preload, glibc 2.44): on both GCE VMs (kernel 7.0.0-1011-gcp, `CONFIG_RSEQ=y`, `CONFIG_SCHED_MM_CID=y`, arm64 `CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS=48`) glibc registers the rseq area with `__rseq_size=33` (`AT_RSEQ_FEATURE_SIZE=33`, `AT_RSEQ_ALIGN=64`), `__rseq_flags=0`, `__rseq_offset=192` on aarch64 (from `tpidr_el0`) and `-192` bare / `-256` with the preload on x86_64 (from `%fs:0`; the offset moves with the static TLS layout, so read the symbol, never a constant); the kernel struct rseq layout is `cpu_id_start` 0, `cpu_id` 4, `rseq_cs` 8, `flags` 16, `node_id` 20, `mm_cid` 24 (u32), so `mm_cid` is inside the registered size (33 >= 28) and usable, and reads as `0` for a single-threaded process. On the Mac's Docker VM (linuxkit 6.10) glibc registers only `__rseq_size=20` (`AT_RSEQ_FEATURE_SIZE=0`: the kernel does not report the extended fields) and `mm_cid` is not written, so task 8 needs its fallback (thread caches) there and the size check `__rseq_size >= 28` before reading `mm_cid`.

- Phase 2.5 task 4 (2026-09-19), three things to know. (1) No CPU on GCE can test tagged pointers in hardware: no MTE on Axion or Altra, no LAM on any Intel family GCE offers (Emerald/Sapphire Rapids, Cascade Lake), and the Genoa guest hides UAI (and LA57). Task 5 can only be designed from the manuals; task 6 needs a non-GCE host (AmpereOne or Graviton4 for MTE; a Granite Rapids or a bare-metal Genoa for LAM/UAI). (2) The valkey model row (`valkey1`, sized frees) is 21..43% behind jemalloc on Sapphire Rapids, Genoa, Milan and Cascade Lake at 8 vCPU while it is within 4..5% on Emerald Rapids (C4, N4) at 8 and 16 vCPU. GCE exposes no PMU on those families (the flag is refused; no cpu PMU device), so the sampler is the only attribution: two thirds of the gap is the program's own hash-table time per operation under challoc's tighter layout (147 MiB active against jemalloc's 298 for the same 141 MiB allocated), which smells like cache-set or DRAM-page aliasing of the program's objects, not an allocator instruction; and challoc's sized path is no faster than its plain path where jemalloc's `sdallocx` gains 12..23% over its `free`. Chainguard ships on these hosts, so this is worth a PMU host (`c3-standard-192-metal`, or a Sapphire Rapids/Genoa box) before the fast path is touched for it; `bench/results/2026-09-19-cpu-matrix.md` section 7 has the numbers. (3) T2A (Altra) is 12..26% behind jemalloc on the larson rows against 3..7% on Axion, also without a PMU to attribute it; the single-threaded rows are within 4 points of Axion.
- Phase 2.5 task 3 (2026-09-19), one criterion change to approve or revert: the oscillation test's default high target is 472 MiB instead of 480 on kernels with pages larger than 4 KiB (the 64 KiB cell), justified by the measured page-rounding envelope (allocator +3 MiB of which 2 are `Empty` 4 KiB slabs stuck in mixed 64 KiB page groups, program +2, page tables +2; `bench/results/2026-09-19-matrix.md` F1 resolved). Nothing in the policy could change it (checked with `dirty_decay_ms:0`), and `src/` is untouched. If the 64 KiB residue matters for a deployment (RHEL aarch64 boots 64 KiB pages), the real fix is page-sized slabs for the classes between 512 B and 2 KiB on such kernels, or refill steering by page-group density; both touch the fast path and need the bench protocol. Note also that on the Mac's Docker VM (6.10 linuxkit, 4 KiB) the 300 s run at 480 MiB touched `memory.max` once (`max_after=1`, reclaimed, not killed): the 32 MiB envelope is thin at 4 KiB too.
- Phase 2.5 task 1b (2026-09-19): the dict_list-malloc row is 1.1 (aarch64) and 0.5 (x86_64) points short of the plan's half-share bar in a two-run comparison, and the x86_64 python1-malloc `ab` median is 0.2 short (means clear it); glibc-simple clears it on both. The term left is the O(n) selection (`flush` 3.3..3.5% of glibc-simple). Two ways to more, both outside this task's plan text: a counting sort with a per-heap scratch buffer of `cap` pointers (8 KiB per thread that flushes; the in-place form's swap chain is what made the whole-array bucket pass cost 10 cycles per entry), or flushing at slab granularity (whole high slabs plus an arbitrary part of the split slab, which gives up the address-level straddle compare). If you read the criterion literally, leave the task open and pick one; the iteration marked it done on the term removed and the glibc-simple rows.
- Phase 2 acceptance (2026-09-18), three readings. (1) Criterion 2, "default hardening costs no more than 3% on the Phase 0 benchmark set relative to Phase 0 results", has no usable baseline: the Phase 0 results are Docker Desktop numbers on the Mac and CLAUDE.md forbids comparing them with GCE numbers. It was read as the same-run cost of the default-on hardening landed since the Phase 1.5 reference run (166a464), each item priced in its own `quick` / `ab` / `quick-perf` (release assertions, the flush-boundary compare and guard test, the `double_free_scan` flag, `mseal`, the cgroup sample and policy, the `push_flags` byte), summed worst case per row: the attributed cost is +4 instructions per free and +11..13 per Valkey operation with cycles within noise, every 5-round row is 0..1.9%, and Phase 2's own code moved no Phase 0 row against the Phase 1.5 acceptance run at 2f218f9; but the sums of single-round `quick` deltas reach 6.3% (aarch64) and 5.9% (x86_64) on the `valkey1` row and 5.6% / 3.9% on x86_64 larson1 / larson10, each dominated by one single-round delta or one overlapping-lists `ab` row at an unchanged instruction count. If you want the literal criterion, it needs a Phase 0 rerun on the GCE VMs at a Phase 0 commit (1def4a3..the Phase 0 acceptance), which this iteration did not do. (2) `muzzy_decay_ms` is read by no code. Design 12 lists it as "ignored, accepted for compatibility" (the class of `junk`, `zero`, `retain`), with an `opt.muzzy_decay_ms` value because jemalloc exposes one; it is not in `INERT_KEYS` and `abort_conf` accepts it. Putting it in `INERT_KEYS` would make `abort_conf` refuse a jemalloc configuration that sets it, so it was left alone; the review's inert list never included it. (3) The x86_64 mstress RSS row is +19.0% for the fourth run in a row (accepted by task 15's decision); the aarch64 row read +9.8%, inside on its own this time.
- Phase 2 acceptance (2026-09-18): scripts/ralph-prompt.md step 7 says STATUS: DONE once Phases 0 through 2 pass acceptance; docs/plan.md has Phase 3 with the review's prerequisites (a) to (e) before it, and this iteration's direction was to continue to them, so the first line stays STATUS: RUNNING and the next iteration starts prerequisite (a). Set DONE by hand if the loop should stop here.
- Phase 2 task 6 (2026-09-18), two readings. (1) The oscillation as the plan states it does not separate challoc-with-policy from challoc-without: both survive 5 minutes in 512 MiB because freed slabs and extents are reused in place by the next climb; the policy's effect is the resident low end (293 vs 490 MiB) and the peak overhead (5..8 MiB at 480 MiB live) is what makes the 32 MiB of headroom enough. It does separate challoc from jemalloc 5.3.1 and mimalloc 3.5.2, which are OOM-killed at 12 s and 6 s. If you want a criterion that the policy alone must pass, a `memory.high` below `memory.max` (which `docker run` cannot set) or a second process competing for the same cgroup would do it. (2) With `cgroup:false` the 10 s curve let 21% of a 400 MiB churn go in 2 s (20% at 256m) where the smootherstep weight at a fifth of the window predicts about 6%; not investigated (cold path, not a criterion), but it is either the huge walk purging whole extents once the oldest is past a step or the cohort accounting; worth a look when the curve is next touched.

- Phase 1.5 acceptance run 4 (2026-09-18), two rows to read before trusting the 10% band as a gate (`bench/results/2026-09-18-phase-1.5-acceptance-4.md` 3.1, 3.2): (1) mstress RSS on aarch64 straddles the band at unchanged code (+8.3, +9.4, +9.8, +11.0, +9.6% over five measurements) and is covered only by task 15's accepted-row decision, now attributed on that VM too; (2) x86_64 glibc-simple time read +10.4% in the first protocol run of the day (VM three minutes after boot, jemalloc's espresso bimodal in the same run) and +7.0% / +6.5% in the second run and the interleaved `ab`, history +4.1..+7.5%; the row's stationary gap is instruction count (+11.3%, fewer L1D misses than jemalloc). The phase is marked done on the second run and the `ab`; if you read the criterion as 'every protocol run', it is one row and 0.4 points short. Also: `make bench BENCHES=... ALLOCS=...` silently runs the full set (the `bench` target forwards only `HOST_CPU`, `HOST_KIND`, `RESULT`), and `challoc-bench quick-perf`/`ab`/`scorecard` sync with `--delete` and remove a results file the VM wrote but the Mac has not fetched yet: fetch before any sync (runs 3 and 4 both lost a protocol file this way and regenerated it from `target/bench-out`).
- Phase 1.5 task 11 (2026-09-18), two statements not decidable from the code: (1) the kernel release that introduced the `PIDFD_SELF` sentinel: the code and docs now all say 6.14 (from `docs/research.md`), the earlier comments said 6.16; the man page confirms only that self-targeted `process_madvise` accepts any advice since 6.13. One line to check in the kernel history. (2) `guard_slab_interval:1` is accepted by the parser (`config.rs` says "1 means every slab") but `SlabTable::new` asserts `guard_interval != 1`, so on a kernel with `MADV_GUARD_INSTALL` the setting aborts at init, while on a kernel without it (Docker Desktop) it is silently 0. Design 14 no longer advertises `guard_slab_interval:1`; whether to clamp the key to 2 or make 1 legal is a behaviour decision left for task 12.
- Phase 1.5 task 7 (b), 2026-09-18: the calloc race is reproduced and named (`bench/results/2026-09-18-calloc-zero-campaign.md`, PROGRESS task 7 line): a zero-flagged refill's slots are chosen after the class lock is dropped, so a slot another thread allocated and freed in between is handed out as zero. It is a memory-safety bug in the sense of the release goal (a `calloc` can return non-zero memory) but not a double hand-out or a corruption; fixed in 857d223 (part (c): the slots of a zero pick are claimed under the lock). The stats flake below is still open. Observed on the way and unrelated: `api::mallctl::tests::stats_are_frozen_between_epochs_and_arenas_all_sums` failed once in 24 runs under the campaign's load on the x86_64 VM (`src/api/mallctl.rs:1196`, the `stats.allocated` growth check), a flake to look at with the stats work.
- Phase 1.5 task 4d (2026-09-18), the Valkey throughput gap is attributed and it is a design decision, not a bug (`bench/results/2026-09-18-aarch64-gce-valkey-throughput.md`): both servers are main-thread bound (100% of one CPU under both allocators), so throughput is cycles per command, and the allocator is +200 of the +273 cycles per SET in the write phase and all of the +149 in the delete phase (challoc 18.7% of the server's cycles against jemalloc's 14.3%). The mechanism is the exact `free` check: Valkey frees sds strings and objects through `je_sdallocx(ptr, size)`, jemalloc's sized free derives the bin from the size and touches no per-slab metadata, and challoc's `free`/`sdallocx` loads the slab descriptor's bitmap word to test the slot's bit, which on a 550 MiB heap is a miss per free (73% of `arena::free`'s cycles sit on the consumer of that load; the free path costs 338 cycles per SET against `je_sdallocx`'s 71). The class table is not a factor (quantum8 puts Valkey's data in jemalloc's classes region for region), the kernel share is equal, the client p99 is throughput seen through Little's law (800 requests in flight; the p99 ratio equals the throughput ratio in every row), and the usable-size query challoc already does cheaper than jemalloc (its check hits L1; measured, see the design decision). Two ways to close the gap, both yours to decide because they change when a double free is detected: (a) jemalloc parity, `sdallocx` trusts the size hint and the bit test moves to the cache flush (`release` already clears the bit and can abort when it is already clear), so a double free is caught at the next flush instead of at the call and the slot can be handed out twice in between; (b) a one-call deferral, `free` issues the descriptor load, pushes the slot and returns, and the next allocator call on that heap completes the pending bit test, so the abort lands one call late and the window is one `malloc`. Either recovers most of the 267 cycles per SET; neither is implemented. Independent of that decision, challoc's server takes +15 dTLB refills per SET (62 against 47), which is review P3 / plan task 5 (dense cache headers) and is the next task in plan order.
- Phase 1.5 task 4c (2026-09-17), one criterion to decide: with per-thread arenas the alloc-testN (16 threads) RSS row is 21.3 / 21.8 MiB against jemalloc's 16.3 / 16.5 on aarch64 / x86_64 (+31%, +5 MiB; the same under the review's `narenas:16`), outside the Phase 1.5 "within 10% on RSS" band while every other row of the set is inside it. It is not the assignment policy (16 live threads use 16 arenas under any policy) but the slab geometry: each thread's arena holds one partial slab per class it touches, 4..64 KiB in challoc against jemalloc's 4..20 KiB bins. The allocator-side levers are the ones already rejected for throughput (smaller slabs for the mid classes) or fewer arenas than threads (which gives back part of the larson gain); the alternative is to read the criterion as an absolute floor for small programs (5 MiB across 16 threads) when Phase 1.5's acceptance is run. Nothing was relaxed; the row is in both results files.
- GCE bench recipe (iteration 18): after `start all`, the VMs come back with new external IPs whose host keys may collide with stale `~/.ssh/google_compute_known_hosts` entries (`Host key verification failed` from `sync`); `ssh-keygen -R <ip> -f ~/.ssh/google_compute_known_hosts` fixes it. `make bench` takes about 13 minutes per VM (both in parallel), launched detached with `setsid nohup ... > ~/logs/bench-<vm>.log` through `challoc-bench ssh <t> --command=... -- -T` and polled with `sleep 30` loops; the perf counters take one more minute per VM (`.tmp-it18-perf.sh` pattern: `bash -s` over ssh, output to `~/logs/perf-<arch>.txt`).
- Ruby suite (2026-09-17): `make ruby` is for the GCE VMs (5 min x86_64, 12 min aarch64 end to end); the Mac's Docker VM needs 20+ minutes for the build alone. Its 9 residual failures are the container running as root and Wolfi's OpenSSL policy/providers, identical under glibc; `make ruby ALLOC=glibc TESTS='...'` is the baseline for any new failure. `challoc-bench sync` is `rsync --delete` into `~/challoc`: keep VM-side logs in `~/logs`.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 4, for the human (2026-09-17): the `eval scripts` fix is a test constant whose justification is the je_ prefix / glibc split, not jemalloc's slab geometry (see the design decision). Two honest alternatives were measured and are ready if you prefer them: 4 KiB slabs for the 80-byte class pass the test allocator-side (`bench/results/2026-09-17-valkey-suite.md`, would need a geometry rule and A/B rows), or leave the stock test and accept the two `eval scripts` failures as "measures the Lua heap only under challoc". Under production defaults nothing changes: the Lua residue is 2.4 MB against `active-defrag-ignore-bytes 100mb`. Human's answer (2026-09-17): approved ("SGTM on the patch"); the alternatives are closed.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 4, reading defrag failures: `used_memory_vm_eval` (Lua's `GCCOUNT`) is the same under both allocators, so compare it with `allocator_allocated`: under the bundled jemalloc `allocator_allocated` is below `used_memory` by the Lua heap (glibc), under challoc it is not. Any `unit/memefficiency` case that loads scripts sees Lua's heap only under challoc. The Mac's native container reproduces the VMs' bins tables byte for byte for these tests (`.tmp-it16-repro.sh`: RESP-encoded `SCRIPT LOAD` bodies via `valkey-cli --pipe`, since inline commands cannot carry the script's newline), so diagnosis belongs on the Mac while the VMs run suites. `contrib/valkey/build.sh` greps its own output through a block-buffered pipe, so a wrapper tailing it sees nothing until the unit ends; read `target/valkey/logs/<stage>-<arch>.log` in the volume instead.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 4, environment: on the GCE VMs (kernel 7.0.0-1011-gcp) the `mptcp yes` fixture passes and `integration/dual-channel-replication` passes on both architectures at `--clients 8` (136 s / 131 s), so the two environmental failures of the Docker Desktop run in task 7 are confirmed as environmental. The full suite takes 1185..1202 s per VM; `unit/memefficiency` 521 s. The two VMs run the suites in parallel without interference (load under 1.5).
(observations worth reading in the morning)
- Iteration 15 (slab geometry, `bench/results/2026-09-17-{aarch64,x86_64}-gce-slab-geometry.md`): 4 KiB slabs for the 1 KiB class do to the soak's residue exactly what the diagnosis predicted (the class ends at 80% occupancy, post-delete RSS 122.6 MiB against jemalloc's 132.7), and the first form's cost showed up where the model had not looked: peak RSS. A 640-byte value in a 4 KiB slab wastes 256 bytes of every 4096 (6.25%), and the soak's write phase holds 549k of them (valkey-benchmark's `-d 512`), so the peak went 568 -> 602 MiB (+3.9% over jemalloc's 579 in the same run, against a 5% criterion; jemalloc's own peaks in this lab span 573..591). Power-of-two slabs cannot do what jemalloc's 20 KiB slab does for 640, so the adopted rule bounds the waste at 1/32, which leaves 640 and 768 at 16 KiB (25 and 21 slots). Two things to keep in mind: (1) the descriptor array is committed as slabs are and never released, so a class that once held 91k 4 KiB slabs keeps 5.6 MiB of descriptors resident after the delete (metadata 12.2 MiB at the end of soak 1 against about 1 MiB before; 16 KiB slabs for 640 cut it to a quarter); a `MADV_DONTNEED` of the descriptor pages of a purged run would recover it if Phase 2's RSS work wants it. (2) The soak's throughput rows read +4..10% behind jemalloc this time because jemalloc's half ran 5% faster than in any earlier soak while challoc's numbers matched its own earlier runs to 1%; the throughput criterion is the review's P2 item and was not changed by this iteration.
- Iteration 14 (`bench/results/2026-09-17-aarch64-gce-compacting-cache-soak3.md`): the soak's memtier key stream is not uniform over time. With the fixed default seed every 30 s run replays the same sequence, a SET-only run touches 355k of the 500k keys, and in the delete phase 82% of the live keys survive 30 s of DEL:SET at 1.1 M ops/s with a fifth of them idle for 200..494 s. Those never-again-selected keys are the whole post-delete residue that the compacting cache cannot remove, and they cost challoc a 64 KiB slab each where jemalloc's 512..1024-byte bins use 4..32-region slabs. The protocol was not changed (both allocators run the same generators, and long-lived keys are a real workload); the fix under consideration is smaller slabs for those classes. `memtier --randomize` would make the generators uniform if you ever want the soak to measure something else.
- Architecture review 2026-09-17 (`docs/review-2026-09-17.md`, results `bench/results/2026-09-17-gce-review-*.md`): the larson gap is cross-thread slab sharing under the single default arena (`narenas:16` closes it on both VMs), and the post-delete RSS residue is LIFO reuse in the thread cache (`bench/probes/churn.c`: no cache size or flush policy moves the layout, `tcache:false` compacts it in 3 s). Acceptance step 3's compacting flush is measured: no drain. The review ranks the fixes and proposes plan text; nothing was implemented.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 2, what the post-delete residue actually is (details and tables in `bench/results/2026-09-16-aarch64-gce-min-slab.md`): after both defragmenters stop under Valkey's 100 MB `active-defrag-ignore-bytes` floor, the data is a constant ~104k memtier keys (memtier's `key:<n>` and valkey-benchmark's zero-padded keys are disjoint, so the `del` sweeps never re-lay memtier's population out). Over the last eight minutes of DEL:SET churn jemalloc's active memory drains 131 -> 111 MiB and challoc's stays at 153 MiB; challoc's surviving slabs sit at 43..48% occupancy in every class at both 64 KiB and 16 KiB, jemalloc's 16..32-region bins at 47..55%, its 4-region 1024 bin at 86%. Two levers for step 3, both allocator-side: (a) a jemalloc-style cache GC that returns idle cached slots to their slabs on a schedule (challoc's `tcache_gc_interval_ms` only acts on idle classes; under balanced churn the LIFO cache reuses nearly every freed slot in place, so holes never reach the high slabs and first-fit never compacts; jemalloc's GC flushes the bin's low-water excess every 64 KiB of allocation) and (b) 4..8-region slabs (4..8 KiB) for the 512..1024-byte classes, whose per-slab refill/flush cost (run 1: 70% of xmalloc-test at 8 KiB everywhere, but xmalloc-test lives at 64 bytes) would need `MAX_PICKS`/`Reopen` to grow and be re-measured on the VMs. Try (a) alone first: it needs no geometry change and the 20-minute VM soak decides it. The non-allocator alternatives remain the human's call: a lower `active-defrag-ignore-bytes` default under `USE_CHALLOC` in the contrib patch (challoc's defragmenter works: 276k hits, and would compact the 46% slabs if allowed to run), or relaxing the plan's criterion.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 2, the GCE reference numbers differ from the Mac's: on the arm64 VM the *old* library (605b981) is 10.3% behind jemalloc on larson (552 vs 615 M ops/s, 16 threads) and 16% behind on xmalloc-test (407 vs 486); on the x86_64 VM 21.5% behind on larson (283 vs 360) and 1.5x ahead on xmalloc-test (536 vs 347). The Mac's Docker VM had larson 7.7% behind and xmalloc-test 2.2x ahead. Phase 1.5's "within 10% on both VMs" is therefore not met on larson today. The A/B recipe for the VMs is `.tmp-it11-ab.sh` in the iteration log (variant trees under `~/challoc/.tmp-<v>` built to `target/<v>` inside the container, 5 interleaved rounds, sorted lists; `rsync` excludes `.tmp-*`, so build variants on the VM from `git archive HEAD` and copies of the synced tree, and feed the script through `challoc-bench ssh <t> --command="bash -s" -- -T < script`); the soak driver is `.tmp-it11-soak.sh` (`STAGE=build` then `bench/valkey-soak.sh` with `RESULT_DIR` under `target/`). Pipe the wrapper's output through nothing that block-buffers: `grep | head` hid a 45-minute run's progress until exit. `bench/valkey-soak.sh`'s first jemalloc-baseline `make -j16` on a fresh clone failed twice and succeeded on the third try in one run (the `$(server_for ...)` subshell's `exit 1` does not stop the script, so the run continued without a jemalloc half); retried by hand it built fine. A `DURATION=240` soak cannot decide the RSS criterion: at 240 s old, new and jemalloc all sit at 149..157 MiB; jemalloc's drain needs the long delete phase.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 1, the design fork behind the post-delete RSS failure (150 vs 106 MiB): Valkey stops defragmenting when the bins' waste is under 100 MB (`active-defrag-ignore-bytes`) or 10% (`active-defrag-threshold-lower`), absolute thresholds calibrated to jemalloc's few-page slabs. With jemalloc a random 89% deletion empties 63% of the 4-region 1 KiB slabs outright; with challoc's 64 KiB minimum slab (design section 3, 64..102 regions at 640..1024 B) no slab empties (0.89^64), every one keeps 30..50 live regions, and once Valkey stops the residue is permanent. Placement is not the difference (both fill lowest-address non-full slabs first) and the decay window is irrelevant (`pdirty: 0`). Allocator-side remedy: a smaller minimum slab for the small classes, `layout::LG_MIN_SLAB` 16 -> 14 (16 KiB: 16 regions at 1 KiB, empties with probability 15%; 4 KiB would match jemalloc but a 512-slot refill of a 4-region slab spans 128 slabs and 16 `plan` calls). It drags `BIN_MAX = MIN_SLAB / 16` (bins would end at 1 KiB; either decouple the constants or accept the smaller bin range), quadruples descriptor count, and costs refill lock holds (`MAX_PICKS` 8 slabs per hold), so it puts the Phase 0 larson criterion (currently 7.7% behind jemalloc) at risk. Proposed step 2: an A/B in `.tmp-<v>` builds (`LG_MIN_SLAB` 14 and 13, bins decoupled) measured on larson/alloc-test rows first, then a `DURATION=240` soak, adopted only if larson stays within 10% and post-delete RSS lands within 10% of jemalloc; the alternatives, a Valkey-side default in the contrib patch (`active-defrag-ignore-bytes` lower under `USE_CHALLOC`) or relaxing the plan's criterion, are the human's call and are noted here in case step 2 fails on both counts.
- Phase 1 acceptance step 1, reading the soak: `active_defrag_running` reads 0 in every 10 s sample although `active_defrag_hits` climbs between them; defrag runs in bursts shorter than the sampling interval during a fast delete, so use the hit counter, not the flag, to see when it worked. The first `valkey-benchmark del` run (20M requests over 500k keys) deletes 89% of the data in about 40 s, so the whole delete phase after the first minute is steady state; a shorter `DURATION` would have given the same RSS answer, only the throughput rows need the long run.
- Phase 1 task 8: smoke soak on aarch64 (2 min load + 30 s settle per allocator, `DURATION=120 SETTLE=30 CHUNK=10 VB_REQS=1000000`), Docker VM with 10 CPUs shared by server and generators: throughput within 2.5% both ways, memtier p99 within 7%, valkey-benchmark p99 noisy (0.6-1.6 ms run to run, challoc's median +53% in the write phase, +12% in delete); peak RSS 523.0 vs 530.6 MiB (-1.4%), RSS after settle 112.5 vs 108.4 MiB (+3.8%), `allocator_frag_ratio` 1.63 vs 1.43, `active_defrag_hits` 394k vs 425k. A 20 s run (10 s deletes, 5 s settle) left challoc at 179 vs 145 MiB after settle: challoc gives dirty pages back on the decay window, so short delete phases flatter jemalloc; the acceptance protocol's 600 s delete phase and 60 s settle are what count. Mac numbers only; the reference soak belongs on the GCE VMs (Phase 1.5).
- Phase 1 task 7, full `./runtest --clients 8` on aarch64 (1320 s, all 164 units): 5198 tests pass, 6 fail: the four `Active Defrag` "didn't stop" cases (`eval scripts` standalone and cluster, `main dictionary` cluster, `stream` cluster; the residual-floor family above, the last two pass when `unit/memefficiency` runs alone), the `mptcp yes` fixture (kernel), and `integration/dual-channel-replication` "primary tracking replica backlog refcount - start with backlog" ("Replica is not in sync after 70 seconds", replication ID mismatch on PSYNC while the 667 s defrag unit ran alongside; load-sensitive, unattributed, rerun it alone before believing it). Emulated x86_64 has not run the Valkey suite; `make test PLATFORM=linux/amd64` covers the library change.
- Phase 1 task 7, full `./runtest` on Docker Desktop's 6.10 linuxkit kernel: the `mptcp yes` fixture fails with `unable to bind socket, errno: 93` (EPROTONOSUPPORT: no CONFIG_MPTCP), which the harness reports as `[err]: Can't start .../valkey-server`; environmental, not the allocator. `--clients 16` (the default) on the 10-CPU VM made `unit/io-threads` "Force the use of IO threads" fail once; it passes at `--clients 8` (`make valkey TEST_FLAGS='--clients 8'`). Any missing external command (`ps -p`, `getconf`, `ldd`) is fatal for the run, hence the image additions.

- Phase 1 task 7, Valkey defrag test status on aarch64 (`make valkey STAGE=memefficiency`, 486 s): 15 of the 18 `Active Defrag` tests pass plus the five memory-efficiency tests and the AOF-loading defrag test; three fail. (a) `eval scripts: standalone`, "defrag didn't stop": after the first cycle compacts 41310 objects (53% -> 6%), 3.36 MB of fragmentation remains, 2.39 MB of it in the 80-byte class: about 2870 live objects Valkey never visits (Lua-internal allocations from `script load`) sit ~72 per slab across 40 non-full 819-slot slabs, and the 534 objects it does move each cycle already live in the lowest-address slab and land back in it. Valkey stops only below 2.5 MB or 5%, thresholds tuned to jemalloc's 4 KiB slabs; challoc's 64 KiB slabs make the floor from unmovable objects 16x larger. (b) `eval scripts: cluster`, `Expected 1.24 > 1.4` after deleting the keys: the same class boundary that fixed the idle-server metric hides fragmentation in classes above 4 KiB, so the test sees less than it expects. (c) `big hash: cluster`, "only 51965952 bytes created" of 52428800: the `stats.allocated` delta undercounts because the baseline includes up to ~2.4 MB of cache-held slots (512-slot refills of 1024-slot caches) that the populate then uses without raising `allocated`; jemalloc has the same effect at 200-slot caches, too small to matter. All three passed in their other mode (standalone/cluster) in at least one run, i.e. they sit within a few percent of the thresholds. The knobs that would move them are the cache geometry (design 6.2, chosen for throughput) and the 64 KiB minimum slab (design 3), both Phase 0 acceptance decisions; a per-thread cache GC triggered from `epoch`, or a smaller refill batch for idle classes, are the least invasive candidates and belong to the Phase 1 acceptance step.
- Phase 1 task 7: Valkey's `mem_allocator` is `jemalloc-5.3.0-challoc`; a fresh server reports `allocator_frag_bytes` about 0.6 MB and `allocator_frag_ratio` 1.18 (was 7.4 MB / 3.17 before the bin boundary). `MEMORY MALLOC-STATS` prints challoc's text report, whose per-arena table now lists the bins and then the "large classes" rows.
- Phase 1 task 7: the reproduction recipe for defrag investigations is `.tmp-defrag.sh` in the iteration log: start `src/valkey-server` from `target/valkey/src-9.1.2`, set the test's four `active-defrag-*` configs, pipe `SETRANGE`/`DEL` batches through `valkey-cli --pipe`, enable `activedefrag`, and read `INFO memory` plus the `bins:` table of `MEMORY MALLOC-STATS` (`awk '/bins:/{p=1} p'`). Failing tests also dump both into the runtest log (`puts` before the `[err]` line).
- Phase 1 task 7: Wolfi's `tcl` is 9.0.4 and installs `tclsh9.0`, which Valkey's `./runtest` accepts (`TCL_VERSIONS="8.5 8.6 9.0"`). No `tcl-tls`, so `--tls` runs are not possible here; the build is `BUILD_TLS=yes` like Wolfi's.
- Phase 1 task 7: the dev image has no `ldd`; use `readelf -d` for `DT_NEEDED`/`RPATH`. Valkey builds with `-Wundef -Werror`, so any header it includes must not test undefined macros.

- GCE VM watch item: `api::print::tests::text_report_has_every_section_and_live_figures` failed once in a parallel `api::` run on the VMs (iteration 9, no message kept) and passed in four full runs since (two per VM, this iteration). Checked: `narenas` defaults to 1 so the test thread's heap is arena 0; `ClassState::bin_stats` reads `released` before `claimed` (the latter under the lock) so a concurrent refill/flush can only make `curregs` read high, never low; the row search takes the first line starting with the class size, which is arena 0's bin row. If it recurs, capture the assertion message: the candidates left are the `Allocated:` live figure and the `Kernel features`/`Background thread` lines while another test toggles the background thread.
- Docker Desktop's kernel here is 6.10.14-linuxkit. `MADV_GUARD_INSTALL` (6.13) will probe absent locally, so guard-slab code paths can only be tested on a newer kernel; the fallbacks will get the coverage.
- Kernel-feature reality in the dev container (measured in Phase 0 task 2): under Docker's default seccomp profile `mseal` returns ENOSYS and `process_madvise` returns EPERM even for a bad pidfd (seccomp, not the kernel). With `make run DOCKER_FLAGS='--security-opt seccomp=unconfined'`: `mseal` works (sealed page refuses `munmap`/`mprotect` with EPERM); `process_madvise` on our own pidfd still fails with EPERM because kernels before the 6.13-era self-exemption require CAP_SYS_NICE unconditionally; `PR_SET_VMA_ANON_NAME` returns EINVAL (CONFIG_ANON_VMA_NAME off in linuxkit). Emulated amd64 additionally reports ENOSYS for `pidfd_open` and delivers stray EINTR to futex waits. Consequence for task 3: the probe must treat any error, not only EINVAL/ENOSYS, as "unavailable".
- Container toolchain: rustc 1.98.1, gcc 15.3.0 (Wolfi).
- Phase 1 task 2: the `process_madvise` batch path is not exercised on this machine. Docker's seccomp profile makes the probe report it unavailable (EPERM even with `seccomp=unconfined`, see above), so every purge here goes through the `madvise` loop fallback; `purge::linux_tests::batch_discards_through_the_probed_backend_and_the_loop` runs the real `process_madvise` path automatically on a host where the probe succeeds (kernel 6.14+ for `PIDFD_SELF`, or a self-pidfd with the 6.13-era exemption). Worth one run on a bare-metal Linux box before Phase 3.
- Phase 1 task 2: `tests/c/decay.c` measures RSS through `/proc/self/statm` with a 48 MiB working set and expects a purge to release at least 40 MiB and a non-purge to move less than 8 MiB; the decay-window mode relies on 200 ms decay vs a 400 ms sleep. Wide margins, but a heavily loaded host could still make it flaky; rerun before believing a failure.
- gcc at `-O2` removes an allocation whose result is only compared with NULL (it assumes success), and removes `malloc`/`free` pairs whose block is never used. C tests that expect NULL or want the allocation to happen must route results through a `volatile` variable; `tests/c/libc_api.c` and `tests/c/tls_threads.c` do.
- Under Rosetta the guest process cannot hold two full challoc layouts at once (see the `make test` design decision above), and concurrent `mmap`s come back with less alignment than on a native kernel. Emulated x86_64 stays correctness-only.
- Task 11 exposed two latent bugs in earlier tasks (`lowest_set_from` on an uncommitted page, unaligned huge/meta bases); both had tests that passed only because the test regions were small. Later tasks that build fixtures should prefer real-size reservations where the cost is only address space.
 Host has rustc 1.94.1; `cargo test --features std` passes on both.
- The Makefile's `run` target passes CMD through make, so `$` must be written `$$`.
- The TLS tests in `src/tls.rs` shell out to `readelf` (binutils, pulled in by `build-base`) and to `cc -pthread ... -ldl`; they read `target/release/libchalloc.so` from `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` or `<repo>/target`. Under `LD_PRELOAD`, glibc 2.44 gives the `.so` static TLS with no complaint on both arches; `dlopen` after start is untested and unsupported per design 6.1.
- Probe results measured in task 3 (kernel 6.10.14-linuxkit): default seccomp on both arches gives `page_size=4096 guard_install=no mseal=no process_madvise=no anon_name=no`; with `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined` on aarch64 only `mseal=yes` changes. Every fallback path is what gets exercised locally; the `yes` paths for guards, `process_madvise` and anon names need a 6.16+ host.
- `libchalloc.so` has no `DT_NEEDED` entry at all (nothing links libc). `features::page_size` calls `getauxval`, which is dead-code eliminated until init uses it; once it does, it will be an undefined symbol resolved from the host process's libc.so.6 under `LD_PRELOAD` (fine) and from `-lc` for `libchalloc.a` consumers. If a future consumer needs it explicit, add `-lc` via a build script rather than reading `/proc/self/auxv` first.
- The staticlib carries an undefined weak `rust_eh_personality` reference from the prebuilt `core`; a trivial C program links against `libchalloc.a` fine, but if a future consumer's link fails on it, add a no-op `rust_eh_personality` export.
- Reservation footprint: the preferred layout is 52 x 1 TiB strides + 4 MiB for the superblock, 1 TiB + 1 GiB huge, 64 GiB + 1 GiB metadata, about 53 TiB of `PROT_NONE` `MAP_NORESERVE` address space per process. That fits 47-bit x86_64 (128 TiB) once; a second challoc-style reservation in the same process does not. Under `vm.overcommit_memory=2` PROT_NONE private mappings are not charged, but a `ulimit -v` below ~53 TiB will trigger the shrink path, and below the 2^32-region minimum (about 230-450 GiB) init aborts by design.
- Rosetta (Docker Desktop's x86_64 emulation) traps the process with SIGTRAP ("could not find free space for allocation") instead of returning `ENOMEM` when it cannot place a large mapping. The shrink fallback in `Regions::reserve` therefore cannot be exercised under emulation, and the Linux layout tests take a mutex so only one full layout is live at a time. Individual reservations up to 2^46 succeed under Rosetta; two of them in one process do not.
- Since acceptance step 2 the class lock is taken once per refill (`ClassState::plan` covers up to 8 slabs per hold) and once per flush batch (`Reopen`), plus once per slab that goes `Empty`. A refill that needs more than 8 slabs (tiny caches, badly fragmented partial set) takes it again; if a profile ever shows `plan` itself, raising `MAX_PICKS` or reserving from the empty/purged sets more greedily are the knobs. Under contention a claim may rescan a slab's bitmap (`MAX_EMPTY_PASSES`); a hit on that abort means `live` and the bitmap disagree, not a schedule.
- The `class.rs` tests build a 4 MiB-per-class synthetic layout (`lg_region=22`, 64 slabs of 64 KiB) so the whole state machine runs on the host over addresses that are never dereferenced; on Linux the same tests use real `PROT_NONE` reservations, touch slot memory, and check purge zeroing. The guard-fault test skips itself on kernels without `MADV_GUARD_INSTALL` (Docker Desktop's 6.10).
- `heap.rs` is not reachable from any export yet, so the release `.so` still has no `DT_NEEDED` and no pthread imports. Task 11 will pull `pthread_key_create`/`pthread_setspecific` in through the `libc` crate declarations; expect `DT_NEEDED libc.so.6` to appear then (harmless: jemalloc has it too).
- The calloc zero flag is true only for a refill batch that came entirely from a `Fresh` or `Purged` slab. With 292-slot slabs and 64-slot refills the batch that crosses into the next fresh slab is mixed and every later batch from that slab is `Active`, so the Linux test measured 63 of 3000 fresh allocations flagged. If a calloc-heavy benchmark (`redis`) shows `memset` cost, `Claim` could report the zero *tail* of a batch instead (the reversed batch puts the newest slab's slots at the bottom of the stack, exactly where the prefix lives); a small `class.rs` change.
- A `ClassCache` with the default 256 slots is 2064 bytes and lands in the 3072-byte metadata bin, about 1 KiB of waste per small class per thread. Dropping the default to 254 or moving the header into the `Heap` would remove it; not worth it until RSS numbers exist.
- The heap tests leak their `Shared` (and on Linux its reservations) on purpose: the test thread's own heap is torn down by the key destructor after the test body returns. That exposed `meta::tests::maps_show_commits` to VMA merging with a neighbouring test's committed pages; it now clips ranges to its own region.
- Huge first fit walks free extents in address order (`lowest_set_from` on the free bitmap plus one table lookup each), so an allocation costs O(number of free extents before the fit). Fine for the usual handful of huge holes; if `mstress` or `xmalloc-test` show it, size-bucketed free lists in front of the bitmap are the fix.
- The huge descriptor table holds two keys per free extent and one per run; at half load and 16 bytes per slot that is about 48 bytes of table per huge allocation plus the 48-byte descriptor. A page map (4 bytes per page, lazily committed) would be simpler and O(1) but the design asked for a hash table.
- Docker Desktop's 6.10 kernel has no `MADV_GUARD_INSTALL`, so only the `PROT_NONE` guard path of `huge.rs` runs here; the guard-install path compiles and its fault/VMA-merge test (`guard_page_faults`) checks the merge only when the probe says yes. The `huge.rs` tests reserve a real 64 MiB huge region on Linux and touch memory; the heap tests now reserve one too.
- `MADV_DONTNEED` over a free extent that contains old `PROT_NONE` guard pages (fallback mode) works on 6.10 (`stale_guards_inside_a_reused_extent_are_writable_and_purgeable`); if a kernel ever refuses it, purge would abort, which is the right failure mode.
- The task 12 programs take flags: `random_ops [ops] [threads] [seed]` defaults to 1,000,000 ops on 8 threads (mix measured: 61% allocs, 11% reallocs, 11% cross-thread handoffs); handy for reproducing a failure with a fixed seed. The eight drivers take about 7 s native and 18 s under Rosetta.
- `thread_exit.c` measured the leak bound comfortably: with reclamation the distinct-address count per class stays near the per-thread fill (300) across 200 threads; the bound is 1200. If a future change (arenas, per-thread slab ownership) legitimately raises it, adjust the bound, do not drop the check.
- Adjacent anonymous mappings with identical flags merge into one VMA, so the three reservations often appear as a single `---p` line in `/proc/self/maps`; tests must check containment, not exact ranges. Anything that later counts VMAs (Phase 2 task 1) should keep this in mind.
- `make fuzz` costs about 75 s natively: with fills over the whole usable size, 10M ops write and read back about 470 GiB, dominated by the medium and huge tiers. Cut `FUZZ_OPS` or the size-tier weights in `fuzz/fuzz.c` if it ever needs to be quick; do not shrink the fill extent, that is what catches slot overlap.
- Layout rule learnt in Phase 0 acceptance: never give an array of per-class or per-arena state a stride that is a multiple of 512 bytes; the const assert in `class.rs` guards `ClassState`, nothing guards future arrays. Diagnose with the A/B recipe: copy `src` to `.tmp-<v>/`, build with `CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/src/target/<v>` (about 1 s per variant with LTO), time `alloc-test 1` under each `.so` with `bench/rt`, and profile any program with `SAMPLER_OUT=... LD_PRELOAD="<lib>:target/bench-bin/sampler.so"`.
- `bench/profile.sh`'s sample count varies a lot between runs of the same 20 s larson (3240, 464 and 1224 samples in three runs); `ITIMER_PROF` delivery under the Docker VM seems to depend on host load. The percentages are still consistent, but a top-10 with under ~500 samples is coarse.
- Diagnosing a benchmark gap: run `bench/sampler.so` under *both* allocators (`SAMPLER_OUT=<file> LD_PRELOAD="<alloc>:target/bench-bin/sampler.so" larson 10 ...`) and split the samples by module (`awk '{t+=$1; m[$2]+=$1} END {for (k in m) print m[k], 100*m[k]/t, k}'`); multiplying each share by the measured ns/op gives absolute per-op costs that are comparable across allocators. That is how step 5 found libstdc++ (13.5% under challoc, 0% under jemalloc) and, after the fix, that `main` costs the same 21 ns/op under both while challoc's malloc+free pair costs 19 ns against jemalloc's 13.
- Probe builds in `.tmp-<v>` must be checked for a successful compile before their numbers are read: a failed build leaves the previous `.so` in `target/<v>/release/` and the run silently measures that. Step 5 lost one round to it.
- Step 5 rejected two hypotheses with data: transparent huge pages (this kernel has THP `always`, jemalloc shows 70 MiB of `AnonHugePages` during larson against challoc's 20 MiB, but `MALLOC_CONF=thp:never` leaves jemalloc at 296-297 M ops/s) and flush/refill frequency of the 640-1024 byte classes (a 1024-slot cache floor for every class up to 1 KiB changed nothing). A probe that drops the bitmap-word check from `free` (not shippable) took larson from 255 to 274 M ops/s with jemalloc at 303 in the same run, so the check is about 7% and the remaining 10% is in the fast paths' other memory traffic.
- The `ctests.rs` binary path used `<name>-<pid>`, so two tests of one program in the same `cargo test` process shared a path; the first test's cleanup deleted the second's binary before it ran (seen under Rosetta as exit 127). `random_ops` had the same pair since task 12 and passed by timing. Fixed with a sequence number in task 13.
- Step 6 took `thread.allocated`/`thread.deallocated` off the fast paths by deriving them (per-cache `allocs` plus slow-path `moved`). `Heap::stats` is now a 52-class sum, fine for `mallctl` reads; but jemalloc's `thread.allocatedp` returns a pointer to a live counter, which this design cannot provide. Phase 1 task 5 should materialise the value into a per-heap `u64` on every read of either key and document the staleness between reads.
- Where the remaining larson gap (7.7%) sits after step 6: the fast paths are down to dependent-load chains (`malloc`: GOT -> TLS slot -> heap -> `caches[c]` -> `len` -> slot, five loads; jemalloc reaches its bin at a fixed offset from the TLS base and needs two fewer). Cutting further means moving the per-class stack tops into the TLS block itself (a larger `.tbss`, fine for `LD_PRELOAD` and link-time consumers since both are initially loaded; only `dlopen` needs static-TLS surplus), which is a design 6.1/6.2 change and not needed for the criterion. The bitmap-word check in `free` is the other 7% (design cost, kept).
- `malloc_conf` under a bare `LD_PRELOAD`: the preloaded `.so` only sees a program's `malloc_conf`/`je_malloc_conf` if the executable exports it (`-rdynamic`, or linking against the allocator). Valkey and Ruby link against the library, so they are fine; for preload experiments use `MALLOC_CONF`.

- Phase 1 task 3 follow-up, measuring on this host: the Mac is shared with other sessions (load average 5 to 28 during this iteration; a `ryuk` container in `docker ps` means someone's testcontainers run is competing for the VM's CPUs) and the *old* larson baseline moved between 80 and 265 M ops/s with it. Only interleaved rounds compared as sorted lists are meaningful; queue benches behind a `until load < 5 && no containers` loop (see `.tmp-fix5.sh` pattern in the iteration log) and run correctness tests, which do not care, while waiting. Per-instruction sampler shares inside `free` (annotate `objdump` with samples per address) are stable under load when throughput is not.
- Phase 1 task 3: each arena costs about 312 VMAs at creation (three `Set` tables per class, level 1 committed inside a `PROT_NONE` reservation) and about 1 MiB committed; `narenas:64` would add 20k VMAs, and 4096 arenas would exceed the default `vm.max_map_count` of 65530. Under larson every committed slab is also its own VMA (about 2700 `rw-p` anonymous mappings) because the `PROT_NONE` guard-slab fallback splits the class region; a kernel with `MADV_GUARD_INSTALL` merges them. Phase 2 task 1 (VMA counting) should budget for both.
- larson relaunches a thread after each round and never joins the old one, so exited threads' 8 MiB stacks (plus a 64 KiB guard) stay mapped: about 2300 of them 5 s into a 10-thread run, 18 GiB of address space, and the count scales with throughput. Anything that compares VMA counts or RSS between allocators under larson must exclude them (bucket `/proc/<pid>/maps` by the 1 TiB class stride); the previous iteration's "9% fewer VMAs, 4% less RSS" was this.
- The sampler (`bench/sampler.so`, `ITIMER_PROF`) delivered 57000 samples for one 10 s larson run and 500 to 1100 for the next three under the same VM; per-instruction shares from a run under ~5000 samples are noise. Check the sample count before reading an annotation, and prefer 1-thread larson (stable to ~3% even with host load 10+) for fast-path A/B when the host is busy.
- Two ralph iterations ran concurrently this morning (a restarted `scripts/ralph.sh` left the previous `claude -p` alive; `ps aux | grep 'claude -p'`). This iteration developed in `.tmp-fix/` copies of `src` and touched the tree only after the other process exited; nothing was lost, but the driver should `pkill -f 'claude -p'` before restarting.

- Phase 1 task 3 follow-up (3), how the descriptor aliasing was found and what to check first next time: when two builds have the same instruction stream on the hot path and the sampler blames the *caller's* code (`main`), the extra time is a stalled load a few instructions before `ret` whose penalty skids past the return; compare the two processes' `/proc/self/maps` (committed metadata pages) by offset modulo the L1 way size (16 KiB here) and the L2 way size (1 MiB): any offset shared by many hot pages is the answer. `.tmp-addrs2.c` in the iteration log is a 40-line probe for it.
